#4968. 14 Best Early Black Friday Rug Deals to Style Every Room (2026)

Rugs are one of those niche investment pieces that can cost a fortune but will have little to no resale value after your dog has done a number on it (guess which one) or dust and hair have built up a layer of filth on top. All of which is to say, if you’re going to invest in a rug, you might as well get a good deal. Dear readers, now is that time.

Black Friday and Cyber Monday are when all the rug brands converge to roll out their biggest markdowns of the year, with deals from retail giants like Amazon and popular rug-exclusive retailers like Ruggable that make the business of underfoot fabrics its specialty. Below, a list of some of our favorite early Black Friday rug deals (and their makers). We’re tempted to bring these rugs home ourselves since we’re already seeing some designer pieces up to 50% off.

Stay tuned for more news on Black Friday events; we’ll update this guide as more sales are announced.

Design Within Reach is a go-to for iconic modern furniture and decor from brands like Herman Miller and Fritz Hansen. Its Black Friday and Cyber Monday sales are among the few opportunities each year to score investment pieces at more approachable prices. Ahead of this year’s event, you can take advantage of up to 50% off clearance items, up to 40% off markdowns, and up to 25% off Herman Miller products. In the current bargain bin, you’ll find rugs spanning the shape, color, and pattern spectrum (like these two numbers, one from Beni Rugs and another direct from the retailer itself).

Ruggable is one of our favorite destinations for durable and washable rugs, whether you’re shopping for outdoor rugs, kids rugs, or even a doormat for your entryway. The brand’s ongoing Last Chance Hosting Sale rug sale includes up to 40% off select styles, many of which you can throw in the washing machine. Shameless plug: Our AD rug collection is also featured in the sale, including this Hema Persad design inspired by Moroccan textiles and architecture. “It fits perfectly in my space and was super easy to set up—the velcro underlay is game changing since I have an energetic puppy at home and it really holds the rug in place, even when she’s being rough with it,” says Stefanie Lindenbaum, director of project management and activation at Condé Nast, who tested out the rug. She also found it easy to clean after a puppy accident, noting a little soap had it looking as good as new. Other designer collaborations are discounted too, like this Scalamandré tiger-print piece and modern graphic style from Jonathan Adler, available in nine colorways.

Lulu & Georgia’s textured, high-pile, and solid color accent rugs come in rich shades of amber and green, with tufted 100% wool constructions that look and feel luxurious. More than a hundred products have been added to the sale section, like this editor-approved rug designed in partnership with Sarah Sherman Samuel (which you might recognize from Mandy Moore’s Altadena home). “Its contemporary look is neutral enough to align with lots of different aesthetics, whether you’re looking to add some whimsy to a midcentury decor scheme or bring some edge to a cottagecore setup,” shopping editor Audrey Lee shares in her review, adding that it hardly sheds. “The material has an elegant sheen to it that looks and feels almost like velvet, so it’s a dream to walk across, and it even brightens up the room a bit.” Another AD100 designer worth browsing is Jake Arnold, whose hand-loomed wool rug combines modern and traditional elements with tonal color-blocking and scalloped corners.

Known for its easy-care styles and collections sourced from design hubs like Morocco, India, and Turkey, direct-to-consumer brand Revival is one of our favorites when it comes to vintage and new rugs. Right now, you can get 15% off when you buy two rugs, with the offer extending to everything except for custom orders, rug pads, and swatches. If we could steer you toward two, we’d start with this washable vintage-inspired, lived-in rug, which fit right into the existing interiors of senior shopping editor Nashia Baker’s “moody midcentury” Brooklyn apartment. “I went with the ‘ruby’ option, which looks just as rich in person as it does online,” Baker says. “The floral details—my favorite feature—give the rug a modern romance that I don’t see as often in the market.” For something more understated, shopping producer Lauren Arzbaecher recommends the Mori Washable Wool Rug, calling it the “perfect neutral.” “The warm-toned stripes aren’t too loud, and play well against the brown velvet of my corner sectional,” she says, adding that it’s easy to clean and doesn’t shed as much as a typical wool rug.

The internet’s superstore has no shortage of quality transitional rugs for indoors and out, including celebrity-led and interior designer-backed collections. Joanna Gaines’ Magnolia Home collab with Loloi is on sale for a limited time, with deals on pieces like this indoor/outdoor striped rug in a versatile neutral palette. We’re also partial to Safavieh’s natural rug and the nuLOOM’s earthy tones and geometric patten.

We are always here for a Serena & Lily sale, and this year’s is no exception. We found great deals on natural fiber rugs and personality-packed picks like this charming floral number. We highly recommend a deep dive to find the piece that’s right for your vibe, whether that’s a vintage-inspired traditional rug, a breezy indigo blue design, or a hand-knotted wool option.

#4966. The 10 Best Underwear for Women, Tested by a Style Writer

Our favorite pair on this list is 100% cotton.

Women's Health may earn commission from the links on this page, but we only feature products we believe in. Why Trust Us?

As a style writer who tests clothing for a living, I’ve worn my fair share of underwear—from moisture-wicking underwear and leakproof underwear to workout underwear that disappears under leggings. And let me tell you: not all panties are made the same. After years of trial and error, I’ve narrowed it down to the ones that actually live up to their claims.

The options on this list are super soft, comfortable, and flattering under clothes, with a variety of fits to choose from. Most are thongs since they’re the most versatile, vanishing under clothing with minimal chafing. But I’ve also included a few briefs that I surprisingly reach for—which, as a die-hard thong wearer, says a lot.

Out of all the options out there, we love the Only Hearts 100% Organic Cotton Bikini best. The classic shape is minimal and goes with any workout apparel, there are a ton of color selections, and it’s made from a super breathable fabric. Plus, the minimal seams and super-soft fabric are some of the comfiest on this list, whether I have this pair on under tight leggings or baggier sweats.

This pair is also one of our favorite underwear for working out because it has a barely-there feel. The waistband doesn’t ride up, fall down, or dig in, which can sometimes be an issue with bikini-style undies. From gentle yoga flows to intense interval training, it moves with you through every bend and sprint.

For daily wear, I love a pair of 100% cotton underwear, and this one is one of the comfiest pairs I’ve tested. But it’s important to remember that cotton isn’t always the best choice for working out, says fabric expert Cathy Rust. That’s because (while it is super breathable), once it gets wet, it doesn’t dry out as quickly as other materials. Though if you prioritize natural fabrics for your underwear, this is a great pick.

After testing dozens of pairs of underwear, the Amazon Essentials Cotton Bikini Brief Underwear is an affordable choice that doesn't slack on comfort. It's made from cotton and stretchy spandex, so it's extremely soft but also super moisture-wicking. Plus, I like that it has a bit of snap for light compression, but it's not too tight or clingy.

This underwear comes in either a six-pack or a 10-pack, so you get a mega bang for your buck, as the six-pack goes for under $15. It also comes in tons of assorted styles—and the color options are endless.

It’s a bikini-style underwear, so it offers more coverage than a thong, but I love it because I can have these on all day and forget I’m wearing them. They have a higher leg cut than normal, which helps avoid pinching or bunching, even while I’m moving around a lot. I also notice minimal lines under my clothing with this pair, but I wouldn’t quite say it’s invisible.

I've washed this underwear a ton of times and have never noticed any shrinking or a change in quality, which isn't always the case for affordable Amazon underwear. If you're looking for good quality, inexpensive underwear, this pair is it.

Of all the thongs I’ve tried, this pair from On Gossamer checks most of my boxes. It’s the most durable pair on this list thanks to a high amount of nylon in the fabric. Wash after wash, it feels brand new. And, for a synthetic option, it’s extremely soft and comfortable. Additionally, it comes in many different coverage options (think: brief, bikini, high cut, etc), is super stretchy without slacking on shape and compression, and it has a cotton gusset, which is essential for wicking away moisture.

This underwear has plenty of stretch—it can expand super wide but still snaps back for a snug, comfortable fit, thanks to the spandex in the fabric. It comes in sizes XS to XL, but the extra stretch means it can accommodate a bit beyond that, too.To make matters even better, it’s buttery soft, so it literally feels like you’re wearing nothing. It's sometimes hard to find comfortable bikini-style underwear that doesn't dig into the skin or move around because of the amount of fabric on the hips. Plus, since it doesn’t shift throughout the day, I never have to worry about adjusting it.

Cotton underwear is great for wearing all day because it's a naturally breathable material, says gynecologist and Chief Medical Officer of Bonafide Health Dr. Alyssa Dweck, MS, MD, FACOG. Merino wool is one of the best materials to look for in a pair of workout underwear, and Branwyn's Essential Bikini is my favorite merino wool option. It dries out very quickly, even when I’m super sweaty. I love that I don’t have to worry about wearing these straight from the gym to brunch if I’m in a hurry.

Merino wool as a technical fabric is one of the few natural fibers that actually performs well in activewear. It comes from the coat of sheep and other animals, is naturally antimicrobial, and wicks away and absorbs sweat rather than letting it sit on the skin. If you run hot during your workouts, you can trust this pair of undies to dry quickly.

The nearly seamless construction also means no weird pinching or discomfort. The stretch (from the spandex in the fabric) means it moves in every direction without feeling restrictive. I’ve had a few of Skims’ Fits Everybody Dipped Front Thongs for years—it’s that good, I couldn’t just stop at one. Hands down, it’s the softest pair on this list. However, it’s still extremely breathable and quick drying, and kept us feeling cool and fresh during HIIT workouts. Unlike other thongs I own, this one never digs into my skin.This underwear also has a dipped front, which I prefer while wearing low-cut pants or crop tops. In my opinion, every woman needs a good pair of boyshorts—for sleeping, extra coverage under short dresses, layering under jeans for warmth, or even wearing solo to yoga or Pilates. This pair from lululemon is perfect for all of that. It looks just like a pair of biker shorts (so no one will know it’s underwear!), but it has the breathability and moisture-wicking benefits of a great pair of undies.

This short is made from 91 percent modal (which is softer than cotton) and nine percent elastane for the perfect amount of stretch. It feels buttery soft, but the bit of spandex gives it a subtle snap, making it great for workouts or light compression under a dress. The flat seams stay invisible under clothes and help prevent chafing, while the smooth waistband sits comfortably without digging in or showing through.

This short has a five-inch inseam and a super high rise, making it a versatile fit for all kinds of outfits. In my opinion, it’s the perfect length for layering under clothes. When I wear it solo to a workout class, I wish it didn’t have stitching at the crotch—this makes it look more like underwear than workout shorts. But don’t get it twisted—I still wear it to the gym all the time. Plus, it’s moisture-wicking and quick drying, so I never have to worry about feeling damp down there after a sweaty sesh.

#4964. 24 Best Early Black Friday Home Deals, Vetted by AD (2026)

Retailers know that you want Black Friday deals—and that you want them right now. Our favorite home brands like Arhaus and Serena and Lily are already offering deep discounts on bedding, area rugs, and accent furniture. Many also have holiday decor collections filled with wreaths and artificial Christmas trees already on sale. #4961. The Man Behind Fashion’s Most Dazzling Spectacles Turns His Attention to the Art of Light

For as long as he can remember, Alexandre de Betak has been entranced by light. As a 10-year-old, he rewired his Paris bedroom so that visitors had to be buzzed in, greeted by shifting colored lights. He went on to found Bureau Betak, a set design agency that transformed the runway with its megawatt spectacles for clients like Dior, Gucci, Jacquemus, and Fendi. De Betak estimates that he produced “thousands” of fashion shows—including Saint Laurent’s spring-summer 2020 ready-to-wear extravaganza, for which he bathed the Eiffel Tower in golden light, and installations for Rodarte featuring fluorescent and neon tubes.

A new truss light sculpture by Alexandre de Betak, seen here in a 17th-century Swedish castle

Now, de Betak, who sold his company in 2021, is turning the spotlight on his own creative practice. On November 5, he opened his first solo exhibition of artworks at Salon 94 in New York. The electronically programmed Truss Light Sculptures are inspired by the scaffolding and fixtures of his early runway shows and in some cases literally made from them. “That equipment is old-fashioned now, but I wanted to honor it,” de Betak says. “And I liked the irony that I spent so much energy over the years trying to hide it.”

On view at Salon 94’s Upper East Side town house through December 19, the exhibit presents two series. In the gallery’s contemporary space, the first is composed of freestanding and wall-mounted aluminum structures fitted with LED bulbs. The upstairs Stone Room, a historic space with Caen stone plaster walls and arched windows, is the backdrop for a second series of versions in bronze. To make them, de Betak had a French foundry recast each part down to the smallest screw by hand, a process that sometimes required more than 20 molds.

A pair of wall-mounted sculptures in aluminum

The light sculptures, which vary from sconce-like creations to hulking constructions out of a sci-fi movie, have a sly sense of humor and a human quality. This is especially true when they come to life, thanks to a remote-control-operated system that activates light sequences that glow, sparkle, thrum, or react to ambient noise, “whether a whisper,” de Betak says, “or extremely loud music.” For these effects, he drew on his stage work, where he was renowned for cueing, or choreographing, light and sound. “I’m interested in the ephemeral nature of what this equipment was made to show—whether a fashion show or a ballet,” he says. “At a moment when everything is virtual, I think it’s more important than ever to create things that produce a live emotion.”

Salon 94’s founder Jeanne Greenberg Rohatyn observes that de Betak’s sculptures are equally powerful switched on or off. Unlit they remind her of David Smith artworks or Russian constructivism. Lit, and with synced sound, they evoke the theatricality of stage performers. “Alex has spent his career building structures that disappear into someone else’s spectacle,” she says. “Here, the structure is the whole point.”

#4959. Binance France emails announce closure of its users’ accounts on Dec. 31

Any assets left on the exchange will be charged monthly fees starting on Nov. 29, company says.

Binance France is closing up shop in the country. Customers have been notified by email during early November, according to media reports.

All accounts will be closed on Dec. 31, and any assets left on the exchange will be charged monthly fees starting on Nov. 29, Cryptoast, Crypto Briefing and other media reported on Saturday, citing emails sent to users by Binance.

On that date, accounts still maintaining a balance will pay a monthly maintenance fee of 0.025% of the balance, or a minimum of 10 USDC, a stablecoin tracking the US dollar.

To avoid fees, withdrawals must be completed by Nov. 29, the exchange said.

A Binance email cited by Cryptoast said: “Due to the orderly cessation of activities, your Binance France account will close as of December 31, 2026. If you have not withdrawn your crypto-assets as of December 31, 2026, this message constitutes a notification of our intention to consider your account inactive, in accordance with our Terms of Use. This can result in the conversion, transfer or other processing of your crypto-assets.”

Related: Binance’s EU services face scrutiny over licensing exemption: Report

Cointelegraph reported in late June that Binance had notified European Union users access to key services would be restricted after the exchange failed to secure Markets in Crypto-Assets (MiCA) regulatory authorization from a member state before a July 1 deadline.

On Sunday, a Binance spokesperson responded to a Cointelegraph query, saying, “Following the end of the MiCA transition period, Binance is progressively restricting certain services for affected users, in line with the schedule previously communicated to them.”

Though asked specifically about reports similar emails had also been sent to users in Spain and Belgium, the spokesperson made no mention of them.

According to media reports, Binance’s global client base counts at least 300 million customers, while the app was downloaded more than 4 million times in the EU last year.

#4958. Creatine Combo Supplements Can Be Confusing. Here's What an R.D. Wants You to Know

Creatine combo supplements are trending, but are they better than regular creatine? An R.D. breaks down the hype.

Women's Health may earn commission from the links on this page, but we only feature products we believe in. Why Trust Us?

If there’s one supplement I won’t stop talking about as both a dietitian and a consumer, it’s creatine. And while creatine has long been a staple in pre-workout supplements, now you’ll find it in everything from collagen supplements to greens powders to electrolyte mixes. Usually called creatine combo or creatine plus supplements, you’ll also find plenty of influencers raving about them right now. But are they actually any good and worth taking?

Creatine has earned the well-deserved hype as one of the safest, best-researched supplements for improving exercise performance, according to the International Society of Sports Nutrition (ISSN). When paired with resistance training, creatine can also support muscle growth, so I get the appeal of creatine combo supplements. Instead of mixing several powders and remembering multiple supplements, you can get creatine alongside other ingredients you already take. And fewer supplement tubs cluttering your pantry? Even better.

A creatine-and-collagen powder could be a good fit if you already take both supplements, while an electrolyte blend with creatine might make sense if you regularly use a hydration mix. Greens powders and post-workout supplements are other options, depending on your routine and goals. The catch? Not every combination provides enough creatine to replace a stand-alone supplement.

Creatine combo supplements can absolutely be worth trying, especially if they simplify your routine. But convenience alone doesn’t make a product a good buy. At the bottom of this story, you’ll find some recommended creatine combo supplements and pertinent info on them, plus lots more info about what to look for when adding one to your supplement stack.

According to the ISSN, 3 to 5 grams of creatine monohydrate daily is a well-researched dose for improving strength and exercise performance in most healthy adults, and creatine monohydrate is the most extensively studied form.

Some combination supplements provide less than that amount, so check the Supplement Facts label rather than assuming you’re getting an effective dose. For instance, Vital Proteins Advanced Collagen Peptides + Creatine provides 10 grams of collagen and 5 grams of creatine, meeting the recommended creatine dose. However, Ancient Nutrition’s blend lists 5.5 grams total of creatine and collagen on its Supplement Facts label, without breaking down how much of each you’re getting.

Higher creatine doses are also being studied for potential brain benefits. A 2025 study explored 10 grams daily, but there’s no established dose for cognitive health yet. If brain benefits are your goal, a stand-alone creatine monohydrate powder may be more cost-effective and easier to dose.

Creatine’s benefits depend on consistent use, even on days you don’t exercise. That’s because creatine works by gradually increasing and maintaining creatine stores in your muscles, not by providing an immediate boost after a single serving.

So, think about how a combo supplement would fit into your daily routine. A collagen powder containing creatine that you stir into your coffee every morning might make it easy to stick with.

Just because a product contains several popular ingredients doesn’t mean it provides useful amounts of each. Check the doses, whether those ingredients fit your goals, and whether you’re paying for anything you don’t really need.

A creatine-and-electrolyte mix may be worth the spend if you need both. But if you rarely sweat heavily or already get enough electrolytes through food and drinks, you might be paying extra for something you don’t need.

The same goes for pre-workouts. I’ve seen plenty of products with impressive-looking ingredient lists that provide less than the amounts typically studied.

Lastly, compare the cost per serving with buying creatine and the other supplements separately. For perspective, one study found that plain creatine monohydrate products cost an average of just $0.60 per 5-gram serving. By comparison, Vital Proteins Advanced Collagen Peptides + Creatine costs about $1.39 per serving. Sometimes the added ingredients and convenience are worth the extra cost. Other times, plain creatine monohydrate is the better deal.

My final take? If a combo provides a full dose of creatine monohydrate, includes other ingredients you’ll use daily, and fits your budget, go for it! Otherwise, I’d keep it simple: plain creatine monohydrate is affordable, effective, and easy to add to just about any routine.

Vital Proteins Advanced Collagen Peptides + Creatine

Legion Recharge Creatine Post Workout Recovery Powder

#4957. Here’s what happened in crypto today

Need to know what happened in crypto today? Here is the latest news on daily trends and events impacting Bitcoin price, blockchain, DeFi, Web3 and crypto regulation.

Today in crypto: Binance France is closing up shop in the country, as all accounts will be closed on Dec. 31. The US Treasury imposed financial sanctions on eight Canadian money-service companies it claims have done business with sanctioned ‌Iranian entities, and the Treasury also moved to cut MEXC off from the US financial system over alleged illicit transactions.

Binance France is closing up shop in the country. Customers have been notified by email during early November, according to media reports.

All accounts will be closed on Dec. 31, and any assets left on the exchange will be charged monthly fees starting on Nov. 29, Cryptoast, Crypto Briefing and other media reported on Saturday, citing emails sent to users by Binance.

On that date, accounts still maintaining a balance will pay a monthly maintenance fee of 0.025% of the balance, or a minimum of 10 USDC, a stablecoin tracking the US dollar.

To avoid fees, withdrawals must be completed by Nov. 29, the exchange said.

The US Treasury imposed financial sanctions on eight Canadian money-service companies it claims have done business with sanctioned ‌Iranian entities.

These companies facilitated the exchange of Iranian rials and other currencies for digital assets, enabling international transactions, a US Treasury Department statement said on Friday.

The sanctions, which serve to isolate the companies from the dollar-based financial system, are the latest in a series of Treasury moves against firms outside the US.

These businesses have facilitated transactions with sanctioned Iranian digital asset exchanges, as well as with digital asset addresses associated with Iran’s Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps (IRGC), according to the Treasury.

The US Department of Treasury on Friday issued an order to cut cryptocurrency exchange MEXC Global off from the US’ financial system after accusing it of facilitating at least $16 billion in transactions linked to sanctions evasion, cybercrime and other illicit activity.

The Treasury’s Financial Crimes Enforcement Network (FinCEN) asserted that MEXC handled transactions involving sanctioned groups in Iran, Russia and North Korea between May 2021 and May 2026. This included around $1.5 billion in transactions linked to Russian sanctions evasion, according to the official press release.

The order effectively bans US financial institutions from processing money transfers involving MEXC and will take effect 30 days after it is published in the Federal Register.

“Financial institutions cannot have it both ways: Either you support the Iranian regime, or you have access to the U.S. financial system,” said Secretary of the Treasury Scott Bessent. “Treasury will continue to sever the Iranian regime’s economic lifelines and thwart its desperate attempts to circumvent U.S. sanctions using digital assets.”

The action is part of Operation Economic Outcast, a US Treasury campaign launched in August with aims to cut off financial entities that help Iran evade sanctions.

According to DefiLlama, the exchange held about $4.48 billion in total assets as of Friday. Cointelegraph contacted MEXC for comment but had not received a response by publication.

#4955. How to Organize a Kitchen Pantry in 6 Easy Steps, According to Experts

A pantry is among the most hardworking areas of the home, but without proper skills, it can also turn into the most cluttered. Learning how to organize a kitchen pantry like a pro requires a pro, so we tapped expert organizers to share their best pantry organization tips. Ahead, you’ll find a step-by-step guide to organizing your pantry—and keeping it that way. The process includes decluttering expired items, grouping pantry staples by category, deciding what to place on upper and lower shelves, and utilizing food storage containers and labeled bins. Whether you have a spacious walk-in pantry or you’re working with limited shelf space, use these pantry organization ideas to maximize vertical space, function, and style.

Start by removing everything from your pantry. “As you are taking the items out, take note of expiration dates and toss anything that is past its best by date, that is stale, or is no longer edible,” says Megan Barnhart, a professional organizer and founder of Barnhart Home. Since the pantry is rarely empty, take the opportunity to deep-clean it—wipe pull-out shelves, wash pantry storage vessels, and sweep the floor. You can also use this time to place liners or contact paper on the shelves. “Liners prevent damage to the wood and paint and make future clean-ups so much easier,” says Catherine Benson, a cleaning and organization aficionado based in Lexington, Kentucky.

Removing everything from the pantry will help you survey what you have in order to organize it better.

Before you return everything into the pantry, group items by category. There are several ways to do this, and experts recommend choosing the method that best suits you. One idea is to group like items together. “Think of your pantry as your own personal grocery store—canned goods together, pastas together, baking ingredients together,” Barnhart shares.

Another option is to categorize items by how you shop and plan meals. “If you shop with specific meals in mind and like to meal plan, group those ingredients together; for example, create a dinner zone that includes individual categories or bins such as pasta, grains, and soup,” says Lisa Eckerle, a professional organizer and owner of The Designer Organizer. If you shop by stocking up on pantry goods, categories like canned goods, baking supplies, and condiments may make more sense. “We normally use a hybrid approach for most of our clients, creating zones based on activity and categories within the zones based on food type,” Eckerle shares.

Once you’ve organized your categories, you might want containers to hold them. But before purchasing any storage vessels, measure pantry shelves, noting their width, depth, and height. Barnhart and Eckerle recommend incorporating the following storage solutions to maximize space and add style.

Risers are one of Barnhart’s favorite ways to store canned goods. “It makes it much easier to see the canned items in the back as well as what is in the front, and I feel it also helps avoid over-buying or repurchasing because you can see what you actually have,” she says.

Turntables are a must-have for pro organizers. “With all L-shaped pantries, we always start by placing turntables in most of the corners; these help maximize that space, avoid food getting lost or spoiled in the back, and create a cohesive visual,” Eckerle says. She notes they also work well on pantry shelves above eye level.

Consider containers and other tools to help store items in your pantry more effectively.

Turntables are great for oils and vinegars, sauces, condiments, broths, and spices. “If you have items you use daily, like nut butters, honey, and spreads that do not require refrigeration, consider putting those items on their own lazy Susan as well; it makes it easy to grab what you need and also makes it easy to clean up small spills and drips because they are contained to the lazy Susan,” Barnhart says.

Barnhart suggests using beautiful baskets or clear containers to group together things like snacks and dry goods. “This contains the items so they are not spilling out onto the shelves or floor and it is also much more aesthetically appealing as well,” Barnhart says.

One of Eckerle’s favorite recent hacks are wire cooling racks on the interior bottom of wooden storage bins. “By adding a plastic shelf liner under the cooling rack for easy cleaning, we created a unique fruit bin that was able to match the rest of the pantry’s decor and still store the fruit correctly with proper airflow,” she shares.

Now it’s time to add everything back into the pantry. “The most-reached-for categories should be near the door or center, with back stock and lesser-used category items placed in the back or the sides,” Eckerle says. She suggests putting meal-related items in the most accessible location, especially breakfast items, so they’re easy to grab when you’re in a rush.

Avoid storing seldom-used or heavy, small appliances on the top shelf or floor. “To prevent injury, damage, and frustration, those items should be stored between knee and shoulder height,” Eckerle says. Additionally, consider at what height you want each category. For example, “whether you want items out of reach of young children or dogs, or accessible and at eye level for children,” Eckerle says.

Keep things you grab often towards the front, while less-used items can go in the back.

“I am a big fan of labeling in the pantry—it helps systems stay in place when everyone knows what goes where and where to put it back,” Barnhart says. A label maker works great for this, but even stickers and sharpies will do.

Once you know how to organize a kitchen pantry, it’s important to maintain it. Eckerle recommends lightly resetting the pantry after every grocery haul. Additionally, she advises scheduling a day each quarter for a thorough pantry reset. “Re-check expiration dates, dispose of open food, and throw away or donate the items/categories that are no longer on your menu,” she says. You can also use this time to consider any seasonal menu changes that may impact how you organize your pantry. “Depending on the season, you may purchase different ingredients or cook different types of meals,” says Shweta Thota, a lifestyle content creator and Intel hardware engineer based in Sacramento. “This may require rearranging your pantry to accommodate seasonal items.” For example, in the winter, you may want to have a setup that works for more canned goods.

#4952. 18 Best Leather Sofas of 2026: Couches That Are Editor-Approved

The best leather sofas adhere to the ABCs of great seating: agnostic design, beautiful texture, and classic silhouettes. Generally, genuine leather is lauded for its durability and longevity—it’s only going to look better with age and some patina. The fabric also makes a wildly comfy, easy-to-clean surface where dirt and dust can be wiped away with a cloth.

If you’re on the search for a new sofa that radiates sophistication (and not just single energy), there are a bounty of leather options at every price point, whether you prefer the softness of suede or the supple, lived-in feel of top-grain leather. Ahead, we gathered our favorite leather couches available online, from small-space designs to larger sectionals that will become the anchor for your future hangouts.

We also included a few wish-list-worthy collectible pieces from midcentury-modern makers like Tobia Scarpa and de Sede, in case contemporary silhouettes don’t quite measure up to the look you’re going for. Below, some of the most handsome leather styles on our wishlist.

Castlery Jonathan top-grain leather sofa is overstuffed, inviting, and supportive—the holy trinity of comfort. We love the inviting modular design that’s perfect for kicking your feet up on the nearby coffee table or simply lounging around in your modern living space. Rachel Fletcher, AD commerce director, has had the Castlery’s Jonathan sectional since 2022 and still gives it an A. The sofa is streamlined—it’s legless look resembles a seat you might find at one of those dine-in theaters. It’s overstuffed and inviting, but still supportive. Contributor Sarah Madaus said it was so comfortable, she asked her partner to cook dinner so she could keep sitting in it. Choose from caramel or warm taupe colorways—or maybe get both, one for the living room and one for the den.

You won’t regret investing in the Article Cigar leather sofa, which has the “soft buttery” rawhide tan texture of a baseball glove, but the rounded curves of a marshmallow. “The Cigar sofa’s back is on the lower side, but I cannot emphasize enough how perfect it is for a New York City apartment with a doorway so narrow it is functionally a nightclub bouncer for furniture,” says digital features editor Charlotte Collins. She noted in her review that the substantial seat padding doesn’t leave any indents and that the silhouette is deep enough to comfortably enjoy a nap. She compares it to “an heirloom piece repurposed for modern interiors.”

The seat on this DWR sofa is leveled up by aluminum legs, so it doesn’t look bulky in the way other grounded designs do. We appreciate the modern details such as the minimal lines and cubic silhouette that inspired Torbjørn Anderssen and Espen Voll, the Nordic designers who created the Outline sofa exclusively for Muuto. It comes in as many as 25 different leather variations that include neutrals like the more traditional cognac to the more design-forward mink and birch. For a smaller footprint, opt for the 67-inch-wide loveseat.

Slim, stilted iron legs give this sofa some midcentury flair—a sound choice of silhouette if you want something trend-resistant that can adapt from one home to the next. Refined details like double-stitched seams and aniline-dyed leather give it staying power. And while a lower back makes it less appealing as a movie night staple, it’s deep enough for occasional snoozing, or serving your reading sessions very well.

Low-slung and clean-lined with a platform base, the Bauhaus-influenced Henley sofa would fit well in a stylish penthouse or add flair to a modern living room flush with chrome accents. The mixed-material sofa’s supple leather softens its industrial-looking stainless-steel foundation. Its tufted backrest brings a sophistication to the design, and slimmer armrests add polish to the overall silhouette.

Not only do AD editors love Quince bedding, but we also have a sweet spot for this Italian leather couch. The low-profile design is classic but not boring, and able to evolve with your design tastes. With two comfy chaises, the design is especially well-suited for spaces where lounging or a conversation pit–style seating is the main goal. Atop the sturdy wooden frame sit layers of plush, high-density foam and removable cushions. Solid wood block legs just peeking out from below complete the look.

This reclining sofa from Crate & Barrel’s Drift line has won over many satisfied reviewers who appreciate that the deep-seated model has a contemporary look and a generous amount of cushioning. Design-wise, the Drift Power Triple is clean-lined, with wide arms, waterfall curves, and camouflaged recliner mechanisms that keep the overall appearance streamlined. The couch’s bold proportions make it a worthy statement piece in your living room or screening space, with especially inviting elements for Netflix binge-a-thons.

Some pieces of furniture just look like they’ll age well, and such is the case with this tufted design from CB2’s Gianfranco Frattini collaboration. An influential architect and designer who helped shape the 1950s and 1960s Italian design movement, Frattini’s ethos is apparent in this sofa, which reimagines traditional elements like tufting for modern tastes. In an unexpected hue like grey, the sofa has the potential to bring richly layered Italian design to any living room.

This classic couch is available in forty different genuine and faux leather options if you’ve been having trouble finding the perfect shade of olive green or a chestnut without red undertones. If that’s too much decision fatigue, we recommend going with a versatile shade of black, mostly because the slim arms bear a resemblance to the luxuriously lived-in black leather sofa we spotted in Walton Goggins’ upstate New York retreat.

Cushy doesn’t even begin to describe the Grantham sofa, whose substantial silhouette can easily take on the load of grounding your entire living room’s design scheme. If lounging is your main goal, extra padding on the already oversized arms eliminates the need for spare throw pillows, while the equally cushioned backrest makes it inviting for future movie nights.

The silhouette of this piece, a collaboration between furniture brand Four Hands and Los Angeles–based interior designer Amber Lewis, feels like an updated take on your parents’ bulky Chesterfield sofa. The built-in cushioning helps give the sofa a more elongated look and, while it could feel too formal for casual spaces, the front legs are fitted with tiny, charming brass casters that bring some unexpected levity to the silhouette.

While a lot of leather sofas look like clones of one another, this three-seater from Soho Home stands out among the pack thanks to its handsome wood frame and casually elevated feel. A shade like chestnut or sangria blend in more seamlessly with the oak wood for a more understated look, while the moss, honey, or classic black hues accentuate the soft edge frame to create a statement piece for your living room.

Maiden Home’s modular Jones Sofa has versatility on its side. Not only is it available in various leather styles and a range of hues, but its pieces are detachable so you can rearrange them to align with your seating needs. Not feeling a three-seater anymore? Convert the set into a loveseat and an armless chair. The low slung sofa draws its design from 1970s influences, with oversized cushions and plush arms. Tailored flange seams add structure to the seams, achieving sophistication without lacking comfort.

Personality is one thing that this vintage de Sede leather couch has no shortage of. Just looking at it makes us want to drape ourselves over the U-shaped frame. Manufactured in Switzerland, the style is all about capturing mid-20th-century craftsmanship that lends itself to comfort. Retro vibes are not to be overlooked.

In a sea of black, brown, and tan leather sofas, Sixpenny’s curved Camino provides you with an opportunity to branch out with its playful “North Atlantic” shade of blue. At the same time, the durable, top-hide nubuck leather is well-suited to handle long naps and family movie nights.

For one, leather couches are durable as hell. Leather is said to last four times longer than a fabric sofa, not to mention the patina of aged leather is currently in vogue. It’s worth it: Durable leather furniture is going to stick with you for the long run. Plus, leather is hypoallergenic. Whatever type of leather you choose, no open fibers means no catching and holding dust or other allergens. (Take that, pet hair!)

If properly cared for, your leather sofa can last a good 15 to 25 years (or even longer)—just look at all the high-quality de Sede designs, Bellini configurations, and Togos on the vintage resale market. Bonded and faux leather sofas have a shorter lifespan (roughly three to seven years). Read on for more tips and pointers on proper leather care.

Dust your leather couch with a dry cloth regularly. You’ll also want to clean any spills or messes up immediately to prevent liquids or oils from sinking into the material. Just like our own skin, you should keep leather moisturized to maintain its vitalized appearance. Invest in a nice leather conditioner and give your furniture a good rub down once or twice a year to maintain its suppleness and prevent cracks, stains, and any other imperfections from worsening. For darker colored hides, keeping your sofa out of direct sunlight will also help resist fading. Read our explainer on how to clean leather furniture if you want to learn more ways to maintain your shiny new couch.

Believe it or not, there are many categories of leather. Full grain describes the most authentic version of the material—only the animal hair has been removed from the hide and has been naturally dyed. This type of leather is a bit tough at first, but it wears into a deliciously soft material after time. Top-grain leather is a popular option, as it’s been buffed to have a softer look (and feel) from the first sit. Split grain is a less expensive construction, as the first layer of leather has been removed. Bonded leather is another one you’ll see out there; it’s made from scraps of other projects and connected with an adhesive. No matter which one you choose, you’ll get that leather look and smell—it’s a great material for all budgets.

The world of leather sofas is expansive. With most furniture, we find successful decorating is less about the actual material and more about the silhouettes, which is certainly still the case here. Beloved for its versatility and durability, leather can evoke moodier interior vibes or more streamlined setups. Darker hues like black, chocolate brown, or navy blue signal the former—our forever muse Lenny Kravitz knows this better than anyone. Lighter tans, beiges, and taupes can feel cozy, like the vintage-looking couch we saw in this Brooklyn apartment with lots of sunlight. Since it’s one of the most popular upholsteries for modern furniture, the list of available sofa styles is endless: From classic midcentury-modern shapes with vintage appeal to softer, low profile designs that can outfit modern spaces like Devin Booker’s living room. In brighter hues, leather sofas can even be true statement pieces, like the textural scene-stealer in this Parisian apartment.

#4951. Air holes turn silicon wafers into flat ultraviolet focusing mirrors for compact optics

Traditional lenses and mirrors focus light using curved surfaces. Metasurfaces can achieve similar functions with a flat layer of nanoscale structures that locally control the phase of light. Extending these flat optics into the ultraviolet could support compact systems for high-resolution imaging, spectroscopy, and sensing, but it introduces a fundamental materials problem: many of the materials used to make metasurfaces strongly absorb UV light.

#4949. An Easy 3 Miles with On’s Cloudsurfer 3

After years of stubborn loyalty to another brand, could one city jog make a longtime runner reconsider his shoes?

After just a mile in On's Cloudsurfer 3, it dawned on me—I’ve been far too hard-headed when it comes to my choice of running shoes.

For years, I held steadfastly to a particular brand and style, year after year, run after run. When one pair wore out, leaving my shins aching and feet tender, I’d quickly order a replacement online without even a thought of pivoting. Different colors and patterns kept it interesting, but that was the extent of my athletic audacity.

Truthfully, it wasn’t about brand loyalty. I wouldn’t dare try another shoe by the same company. It was more a case of preferring what I had grown used to in the past decade over the potential thrill of experimentation.

When I was asked to take On’s Cloudsurfer 3 for a jaunt, I was skeptical. Not that the shoes couldn’t or wouldn’t be great—it’s On, after all, a company renowned for exceptional technology and an ultralightweight feel to their sneaks—but that they’d be able to win over someone like me.

Still, I was game. The assignment called for my honest opinion during a three-mile run, and I was ready to break out of my rut (or at least try to). So I laced up and headed out on a beautiful early-fall Sunday afternoon in New York City. Here’s how it went.

I was intrigued even before I took my first step. Putting on Cloudsurfer 3, it immediately felt lighter than my go-to shoes. That surprised me, considering the minimal weight of my faves is what originally attracted me to them. The knit-mesh on the outside of the Cloudsurfer 3 was light and airy, and the inside was plush in all the right places (including the generous ankle collar) without being overbearing.

Yet, I knew the bigger test was to come. As a long-time sand volleyball player, I’ve developed a harder-than-necessary plant of my foot when I run, so I tend to need soft cushioning from a shoe.

Heading outside, I took a breath and set off down the sidewalk. The first strides were cautious, as I focused on the new sensation. The initial feedback was encouraging. It all felt effortless and soft, which I later would learn was due to On’s proprietary Surreal™ Superfoam with CloudTec®. I knew of On’s “run on clouds” tagline, but now I was feeling it in action, and I liked the smooth, soft ride I was experiencing.

I was a half-mile in when I hit my first red light—an unavoidable hazard of city running—giving me a moment to stop and reflect. “That was easier than usual,” I thought. I appreciated the secure feel of my ankle without it being too constricting, and my foot didn’t shift at all in the propulsive phase of my gait. The toe box was nice and comfy.

Pleasantly surprised so far, I changed my route on the fly and headed into Central Park. I wanted to test out the Cloudsurfer 3 on the oval around the Great Lawn . . . and enjoy an uninterrupted 0.55-mile loop.

I was breathing a little heavier at this point, but I noticed something about the shoe: It gave me the sensation of running downhill, even on a flat surface. I also felt what I’d call a “rebound” effect as each foot touched down and lifted off. This energy return made me feel faster than usual, with a burst of extra power on my “swing” phase and increased confidence in my mechanics. Could the Cloudsurfer 3’s mere 10.8 ounces per shoe be the difference maker?

I don’t know if it was the refreshing chill in the air, the clearer path than I’m used to in the city, or an extra “oomph” that the shoes’ softness provided, but I did a doubletake when seeing my 10:02 split time at the end of the second mile—well ahead of my typically slow 11-minute pace.

After two treks around the Great Lawn, I was back on the paved trail that leads out of the park. The sweat was beading despite a comfortable temperature in the high 60s, but I was enjoying the workout.

By the last mile, my legs were feeling good thanks to the easy two miles prior. I was in “go” mode and barely noticed my shoes. I decided to switch up my routine and finish with a series of sprints on the city blocks—fast bursts between intersections, with short rests at red lights.

Everything was still flowing smoothly: an effortless “lift” on each step, a seamless heel-to-toe transition, and no reverberations from the hard sidewalk and street underfoot. The last third of the run seemed to fly by, and I was more than happy with yet another sub-11-minute mile. I also realized that a fresh scrape on my ankle from a volleyball match earlier in the week, which I had been worried about aggravating during the run, was completely pain free; there was no chafing whatsoever.

When I took this assignment, I knew I’d likely be impressed with On’s Cloudsurfer 3. This is no ordinary sneaker, after all, but one designed with Swiss precision and an array of features celebrated by regular Joes and top-tier marathoners alike.

What I wasn’t quite ready for was the thought that maybe, just maybe, it was time to move on from my old standbys. I do know one thing as I reflect on the experience: My daily runs will be in On’s Cloudsurfer 3 because its soft, smooth ride made this run feel effortless.

#4947. Meghan Trainor. Zedd. Joe Jonas and Sophie Turner. This $18 Million Manse Is Collecting Celeb Owners. Who’s Next?

Meghan Trainor has listed her Encino, California, home for just under $18 million. If the songstress and her actor husband, Daryl Sabara, pass the torch to another notable name, it would continue the estate’s ongoing chain of celebrity stewards. Trainor bought the house from DJ Zedd in 2024 for about $17 million; Zedd had paid $15.2 million in 2021 when he bought it from Joe Jonas and Sophie Turner, who were the first to move into the six-bedroom, 12-bathroom manse when it was completed in 2019. They scooped it up for $14.1 million. To put it simply: The property has never been owned by someone without a Wikipedia page. As its listing description reads: “This compound is for the most discerning Buyer.” Unsurprisingly, the home hosts a swath of amenities that are as elite as its residents.

Tucked behind tall gates and hedges, the contemporary-style build, designed by Jae Omar, spans nearly 15,000 square feet. The dwelling’s main floor hosts a great room with two fireplaces, wall-to-wall pocket doors that open to the backyard, and a library nook. The eat-in kitchen is complete with a built-in bar and a sprawling center island with a live-edge wooden breakfast bar seating seven. An additional catering kitchen, an office, and an impressive playroom outfitted with a climbing wall, a slide, and a foam pit round out the main level, along with a fitness and wellness center and “perhaps the best-sounding studio in the world,” according to Zedd, who added that amenity during his ownership.

Five ensuite bedrooms are situated upstairs, as well as another fireplace-warmed living area. The primary suite boasts yet another fireplace, a wet bar, a bathroom, two spacious dressing rooms, and walls that slide open to a sizable balcony. Outdoor amenities include a pool and spa, a kitchen, a putting green, a projection screen, and a fire pit. A guesthouse with a billiards pub and a movie theater round out the compound. #4945. Billy Joel at Home: 10 Photos of the Piano Man Through the Decades

For Billy Joel, New York is more than just a place. It’s an inextricable part of what makes the singer who he is. “My speech patterns are New York. The music that I like, it was all New York–based. That’s what I think of when I think of home,” he said in his 2025 HBO documentary Billy Joel: And So It Goes. “It’s my roots.”

The Bronx native grew up in Hicksville, a middle-class suburb of Nassau County on Long Island. His family struggled to make ends meet. “There was poor, and then there was Billy Joel poor. He was lucky if he had enough money to buy a pack of cigarettes,” his childhood friend and fellow musician Jon Small said in And So It Goes. According to Joel’s sister Judy, no matter how hard times were, their mother Rosalind insisted he take piano lessons. His talent “had to be nurtured,” Judy said in the HBO doc. “She knew that he was going to be something someday.”

Before he indeed became something, Joel worked on an oyster boat. “I used to look up at this mansion on the hill and wonder what it would be like to live in a house like that,” he said in the documentary, referencing the waterfront property on Centre Island he nicknamed MiddleSea. “I used to think, ‘They’re rich bastards. They never had to work a day in their life.’ Well, I own that house now. It’s not finished yet. And neither am I.”

Read on for a glimpse into the Grammy winner’s life, from his early days in New York to his brief stint in Los Angeles and return home.

Joel’s music career took the Long Island native to Los Angeles in the early 1970s, and he signed with Columbia Records in 1973. After three years in LA, he and his first wife, Elizabeth Weber, returned to his home state, where he completed his fourth studio album, Turnstiles. “I wanted to get back to New York and make more of a rock and roll album,” Joel explained in And So It Goes. “I was on a bus on my way back to Highland Falls, New York. Elizabeth found us a house up on the Hudson River, and it was fall. It was just gorgeous. The colors were ablaze, the trees were, like, gold and red, and I remember thinking, ‘This is the New York State that I loved when I was a kid. This is magic. We don’t have this in California.’ And I wrote this love song to New York on the bus.” It would become “New York State of Mind,” one of Joel’s most popular tunes.

The singer-songwriter poses with some of his records in January 1978.

Joel’s fifth studio album, 1977’s The Stranger, was a runaway success—but the singer, pictured alongside his accolades in New York in 1978, told Playboy a few years later that his newfound fortune didn’t faze him. “I read these stories about Peter Frampton’s multimillion-dollar mansion, 15 Rolls-Royces and all that,” he said. “Who cares? It’s all bullshit. What was his last record like? Was it any good? That’s the question. People get hung up on the money thing. It’s nice to have four motorcycles. It’s fun. And I walk into the garage and go, ‘Holy shit! I’ve got four motorcycles!’ But I don’t go beyond a certain point.”

Joel, his then-wife Elizabeth, and their dog in 1978.

Instead of splurging on a million-dollar mansion, Joel told Playboy that when he and his wife decided to buy a home, “We got a $300,000 house with a mortgage like everybody else.” Their glass house, featuring floor-to-ceiling windows overlooking the Long Island Sound, was located in the town of Oyster Bay on Long Island’s North Shore. Joel appears poised to hurl a rock at the home on the cover of his 1980 album, Glass Houses. “The old thing about people who live in glass houses shouldn’t throw stones—I don’t believe it,” he said. “I think, Why not? Take chances. I really wanted to throw a rock at my own house.”

Joel and Elizabeth at their Long Island residence.

In the 1970s, Joel asked Weber to be his manager. “The company I’m with now is New York–based,” Joel said of his representation. “It’s called Home Run—nothing to do with baseball. It’s a home-run agency.” Weber got right to work. She recalled in his HBO documentary that the “story [Joel] likes to tell” of her early days on the job is that he woke up the next morning to phones everywhere and “cabinets installed.” However, she said, “That’s not really true, but it’s close to true.” The couple (seen here at Joel’s grand piano in their living room) split time between their Manhattan apartment, where they worked, and Oyster Bay. Weber left her role managing Joel when the two split, and her brother Frank picked up where she left off. Home Run dissolved amid financial woes in the ’80s, and Joel eventually pressed charges against Frank over mishandled funds; the parties reached a settlement, the terms of which have not been disclosed publicly.

The musician mixing tapes in the music studio of his New York home.

The Oyster Bay house, a midcentury-modern residence, was not far from where Joel grew up in Hicksville. Photographed here in his at-home music studio in 1978, the singer’s career was on the upswing—but his relationship with Weber was growing tense. “Having a big boom in record success, I think my marriage was starting to unravel around then; 1979, it wasn’t the happiest time,” Joel once said. “Record success doesn’t translate into happiness.” Joel assumed things were paying off financially, but he admitted he “wasn’t aware” how much money he was actually bringing in. “I know we were living in a nice house, my wife was going out and buying all kinds of nice stuff,” he said. “I never really went out and did that.”

Joel multitasks in his LA home studio in 1984.

Though Joel had settled down on the East Coast, he told the Los Angeles Times that his California years cross his mind “all the time.” The singer, pictured here in LA in 1984, wrote his hit song “Piano Man” based on his experience as a lounge musician (using the name Bill Martin) at a now-defunct bar near the corner of Wilshire Boulevard and Western Avenue during the early 1970s. “I wonder what happened to the people I knew—the people in the bar in ‘Piano Man,’” he said. “It was the Executive Room in the Wilshire district. I got tips and made union scale, and I was able to afford an apartment in Studio City. The weather was great; the people were nice.”

Though it appears Joel lived like a rock star in Los Angeles—as seen here in 1984, perched on a sheepskin-style throw over a leather sofa with a pack of Marlboros and a landline telephone by his side—he told the Los Angeles Times in 2023 that he felt more like “an exiled writer living in Paris” during his early stint in the City of Angels. “I went from being an insignificant nobody to the Piano Man while I was there,” he said. “I still look back to my time in LA with a certain amount of romance.”

Joel is pictured here at the dinner table in 2001. When The New Yorker paid him a visit at his Centre Island residence in 2014, he described the interior’s aesthetic as “Catholic martyrs and saints, grotesqueries.” In the library, Joel—who decorated the place himself—had a model of the Queen Mary on his mantel, and in the living room facing the fireplace was a triptych of the Last Supper along with silver jugs and bowls on a nearby table.

Joel’s various properties have passed through some famous hands. He and his second wife, supermodel Christie Brinkley, owned a 12-acre oceanfront property in the East Hampton hamlet of Amagansett. The couple raised daughter Alexa Ray Joel in the Long Island dwelling. It was later sold to Jerry and Jessica Seinfeld for $32 million. According to The New Yorker, Joel also sold a duplex on Central Park West to singer-songwriter Sting for $5 million and a house on Martha’s Vineyard to businessman Mickey Drexler. At one point, Joel purchased a home in the Hamptons from Jaws star Roy Scheider.

“As a little kid, I always liked boats and the romance of living on an island,” Joel told Parade in 2018. The singer—pictured here on an East Hampton dock in 2001—has always felt at home on the water. In 2007 he designed a 57-foot powerboat called the Vendetta that can go about 50 knots, per The New Yorker. Boating represented “freedom,” Joel said in his documentary. “I am the captain of my ship.”

#4944. ‘I’m Entering My Third Bodybuilding Competition at 52. Here’s How I Build Muscle in Midlife.’

“Strength, resilience, and the courage to pursue goals don’t have an age limit.”

Women's Health may earn commission from the links on this page, but we only feature products we believe in. Why Trust Us?

When I was 20 years old, I thought I’d reached the peak of my fitness journey: I became one of India’s first female bodybuilders, and was even acknowledged in India’s national and world records book. It was an incredible honor, and I took immense pride in the discipline, strength, and athleticism it took to get there. Little did I know, though, that some of my greatest fitness accomplishments would come decades later, in my 40s and 50s.

Growing up, I always loved playing sports recreationally, and that passion for movement and competition eventually led me to bodybuilding. But at 24, I got married and moved to the United States with my husband; shortly after, I gave birth to my daughter at 29. With all these big life changes, fitness fell by the wayside. My life was centered around giving to my family, and somewhere along the way, I stopped prioritizing myself. Over the next two years, I became chronically exhausted, lost muscle, gained weight, and didn’t feel like myself physically or mentally.

Then, when I was 33, I decided to make a change. I thought back to the discipline and structure I once had with fitness and realized how much I missed that part of my life. I wanted to get back to those habits and rebuild the strength I once worked so hard for, so, I began making time for fitness.

My husband started watching our daughter after work while I began doing full-body workouts using light weights, focusing on consistency rather than intensity. I already had a strong foundation in strength training from my bodybuilding days, so I created most of my own workouts. But I also reached out to my former coach in India, who helped program some of them.

At first, the workouts were hard, and I was sore every day. But mentally and emotionally, I felt amazing; for the first time in years, I was committed to doing something just for myself. Starting then, I promised myself that I would never stop prioritizing my health again.

Over the next year, I worked my way up to hour-long workouts and continued lifting heavier weights. As a result, I gained significant muscle and lost more than 20 pounds. But the physical changes were only part of the transformation—I had more energy during the day, slept better at night, and felt more confident and comfortable in my own skin. Every workout reminded me that I was getting stronger not just physically, but mentally and emotionally, too.

As the years went on, I kept growing muscle and losing weight by strength training four to five days a week. Still, there was always a voice in the back of my head telling me to try bodybuilding again. So, at 48, I entered another competition. I worked with a coach who helped me gradually push my limits by lifting heavier weights, adding more challenging strength-training exercises, and increasing the reps and sets of my workouts. I also followed a strict plant-based, protein-forward diet (I became vegan for ethical reasons when I was 38). So, I ate lots of tofu, tempeh, lentils, beans, fruits, vegetables, whole grains, nuts, and seeds. The work was demanding, but it was incredibly motivating to watch my body transform through discipline and consistency.

Stepping back on stage again was a deeply rewarding experience, and I felt so proud of the hard work and dedication it took to get there. Most importantly, it was so empowering to show my teenage daughter that strength, resilience, and the courage to pursue goals don’t have an age limit.

I took four years off of bodybuilding since life got busy and I wanted to give my body a break from competition prep. But about a year and a half ago, I decided I wanted to challenge myself again and see what I could accomplish in this new decade of my life. My training approach didn’t change too much since the last competition—I just kept building as much muscle as possible.

In April 2025, I began my “bulk” phase, a period focused on growing muscle by increasing my calorie intake and lifting heavy. I followed a structured workout plan with a coach five days a week through two lower-body days and three upper-body days. During each session, I usually did five to seven exercises, typically for four sets of six to eight reps with heavy weight for muscle growth. I used a mix of free weights and weight machines, and some of my staple moves were pull-ups, leg presses, leg extensions, squats, lunges, chest flies, and lat pulldowns. I also intentionally incorporated pauses at the top or bottom of certain movements to make each exercise more challenging.

Training for a bodybuilding competition also means entering a “cut” phase, which involves reducing body fat so muscle definition can show on stage. So, in January of this year, I reduced my calorie intake while continuing to strength train with heavy weights. After my strength-training sessions, I also did 45 to 50 minutes of cardio on the stair stepper or elliptical. Additionally, I finished every workout with a core circuit to improve muscle definition that includes moves like hanging leg raises, situps, and bicycle crunches.

Recovery has always been non-negotiable for my workouts, too. I lightly stretched after every session, and on my off days, spent at least an hour doing deeper stretching with resistance bands and a foam roller to maintain mobility and reduce my risk of injury.

I swear by this protein powder to support my training and recovery. It’s easy to mix and has a smooth texture without the chalky taste.

These Puma sneakers are my go-to for lifting because they provide a stable, supportive base that helps with balance and even weight distribution.

I love this tofu as an easy way to get more protein. It’s delicious and the taste blends well into nearly any meal, from stir-fries to bowls.

I’ve learned that strength isn’t just about what happens in the gym. Every little habit works together as a system: how I train, eat, recover, sleep, and take care of myself mentally and emotionally. When those pieces are in balance, I have more energy, feel stronger, and perform better.

But instead of trying to change every one of my habits overnight, I took small, manageable steps, like dedicating just 20 minutes to myself at the gym. Once that became routine, I built on it by focusing more on nutrition and recovery, then eventually, I set my sights on returning to the bodybuilding stage. When I treat my body and mind as a whole, I’m able to gain strength sustainably, which allows me to keep doing what I love.

The simpler my workouts are, the more consistent I can stay with them. That’s why my coach and I work on foundational exercises like squats, lunges, presses, rows, and pull-ups. But to continue challenging my muscles in different ways, we use simple adjustments like tempo, pauses, and stance variations.

For example, while doing a squat, keeping my toes pointed forward targets my quads and center glutes. But turning my toes slightly outward helps me feel more activation in the outer glutes and inner thighs. Small adjustments like these allow me to keep progressing with simple and effective movements without constantly reinventing my routine.

For years, I was so focused on caring for my family that I stopped making time for myself. Getting back into strength training changed that. I realized that taking care of my health isn’t selfish—it’s necessary, and making that commitment has given me more strength, energy, and confidence in every part of my life. By scheduling my workouts like any other important commitment, planning ahead, and staying consistent even when life gets busy, I’ve found a way to prioritize myself while still being there for my family.

Today, strength training is about more than building muscle or preparing for a competition. It’s one of the ways I continue to invest in my health, challenge myself, and pursue goals that bring me joy. I’ve learned that you don’t have to be where you once were to start again. You just have to decide that you’re worth the time and effort, take the first step, and keep showing up.

Andi Breitowich is a freelance writer who covers health, fitness, relationships, beauty, and smart living. She is a graduate of Emory University and Northwestern University's Medill School of Journalism. Her work has appeared in Women's Health, POPSUGAR, Food & Wine, What To Expect, Cosmopolitan, Men's Health, and elsewhere. As a former collegiate pole vaulter, she loves all things fitness and has yet to meet a group workout class she doesn't like.
