#4938. The World’s Most Famous Architects Are Building in Albania. I Had to Find Out Why

“I’ll try to focus on this, which is not the easiest thing today,” says Edi Rama, the embattled prime minister of Albania, after a night spent monitoring violent protests in the capital, Tirana. But Rama quickly warms to one of his favorite subjects. “Architecture,” he says, choosing his words carefully, “has the power to change societies. It’s like a strong medicine. It can heal, or it can kill.”

As a journalist who covers architecture, I had been hearing about a building boom in Albania for several years. It seemed like half the architects I knew in New York were on a first-name basis with the prime minister, a working artist and, at six-foot-seven, a former pro basketball player. So on October 19 I flew to Tirana—a short side trip from Greece, where I was on vacation with my husband—to look at some of the new buildings and interview Rama about them. But I arrived during a season of civil unrest. The prime minister, whose assistant had told me I needn’t postpone the trip because everything was fine, had moved to a secure location on the outskirts of the city; some of the buildings I had come to see had just been vandalized by protesters; and at one point I was accused, publicly, of aiding a government spy. (The charge was—need I even say this—absurd.) And, because I’m in the early stages of Parkinson’s disease, I experienced all this from a four-wheeled mobility scooter, which I had rented for about $100.

As I tooled around the city center, much of it seemed unaffected by the protests. In fact, most of Tirana was charming. But weren’t those Molotov cocktails we heard going off during an alfresco dinner? Those weren’t on my drinks menu.

What was on my menu in Tirana, a city of about 600,000 in a country of about 2.3 million, was confusion. Confusion about why the protesters were so angry, and about why the prime minister seemed so unconcerned. In his 13 years in office, Rama, 62, has been remaking as much of Albania as possible with the help of foreign architects. Even the heavily guarded villa where he agreed to be interviewed, a raw concrete building dyed bright red, is largely the work of the Spanish architect Pablo Bofill.

When protesters began demanding Rama’s resignation last May, he was about to host his second annual gathering of architects from around the world, one of the high points of his year. Architects like the Danish superstar Bjarke Ingels and the Chilean Alejandro Aravena, who won the Pritzker Prize in 2016, “pay to come here and talk, and they love it,” Rama says. But this year the get-together, from June 3 to 5, drew protesters with signs that read “Starchitects go home!” Those protests presaged the more violent demonstrations in October and November.

I haven’t attended either of Rama’s conclaves, but what I’ve heard reminds me of my visit to Ordos City, Inner Mongolia, in 2008, which I wrote about for The New York Times. A wealthy industrialist, aided by the artist Ai Weiwei, had brought 100 young architects from around the world to the isolated desert city. Their task was to design 100 villas for anonymous (and seemingly nonexistent) Chinese millionaires. Both situations felt surreal.

Rama normally works in an office on the Boulevard of Martyrs of the Nation in the center of Tirana. But on October 20 the boulevard’s sidewalks were missing hundreds of paving stones, which protesters had used to shatter storefronts. And some of the stones were reportedly thrown at police during nighttime clashes that authorities say left hundreds injured. Police have used tear gas and rubber bullets as protesters set small buildings, like an information kiosk in the vast Skanderbeg Square, on fire. Near the shell of the kiosk, workers scrubbed fresh graffiti off a statue of Skanderbeg himself. The Tirana Pyramid, built in the late 1980s by followers of the brutal dictator Enver Hoxha, and recently turned into a public amenity by the Rotterdam firm MVRDV, was also damaged.

There’s a reason some Albanians—it’s impossible to say how many—resent the foreign architects and the attention lavished on them. In the 26 years since he became the mayor of Tirana and then prime minister, Rama has initiated or encouraged more than 50 international architecture competitions for projects ranging from parks to museums to large housing developments. Forty-eight of those were won by foreign architects (some working with Albanian firms), according to Adelina Greca, who leads the National Territorial Planning Agency, which organizes competitions for the government. Only two were won by Albanian firms working alone. In addition, Rama has played matchmaker, giving private developers the names of foreign architects he strongly recommends they hire.

As a result, Tirana’s compact city center is filled with buildings in bright colors and novel shapes. There is a so-called vertical forest, a skyscraper laden with trees and shrubs, by the Italian architect Stefano Boeri. The green-colored building is just a stone’s throw from the bright red Marriott by another Italian architect, Marco Casamonti. The Hotel Plaza Tirana, by architect Johan Anrys, of the Belgian firm 51N4E, starts as an ellipse at its base and morphs into a rectangle at its crown. But the champion attention-getter is a condo building by MVRDV in the shape of a head. Its façade and balconies resolve into facial features, most conspicuously a pronounced nose. The architects have publicly identified it as the head of Skanderbeg, a 15th-century military hero. But some Albanians think it’s Rama. Both ideas are “urban legends,” says the prime minister. “It’s the form of a man through history,” he says, contradicting the architects’ own statement. Nodding at this reporter, he says, “It also looks like you.”

Tirana’s Rock (a.k.a. the Skanderbeg building) is an MVRDV-designed condo in the shape of a head

A Marriott, designed by Marco Casamonti of Archea Associati, left, and the “vertical forest” skyscraper, by Stefano Boeri

The vertical forest, the Marriott, and the Plaza Hotel are all handsome additions to the city. So, too, is the “head,” but is it too much of an architectural one-liner? Who, I wonder as I pass it several times a day, is going to want to live in the nose?

Johan Anrys, who 22 years ago became one of the first foreign architects in Albania, told me that working there has taught him “what architecture truly means for improving society.” He said he knows that Albanian architects wonder, Are we not good enough? and believe we’re taking jobs from them. “It isn’t true. I have a studio in Tirana with 20 Albanian architects.” Overall he says, the foreign architects have “brought Albanian architects more work than we’ve taken.”

Rama tells me—on-the-record, and perhaps unwisely—that the only Albanian architects who have a problem with his overtures to foreign firms are “the mediocre ones.” The rest, he explains, see that having first-rate architects from abroad, including Herzog & de Meuron, OMA, and Diller Scofidio & Renfro, working in Albania will raise everyone’s game.

Rama tells me—on-the-record, and perhaps unwisely—that the only Albanian architects who have a problem with his overtures to foreign firms are “the mediocre ones.”

The foreign architects who win competitions are required to enlist architects licensed in Albania to help them execute their plans. Rama compares the situation to that of China, which 20 years ago was giving its most prominent building commissions to foreign architects, including some of the same ones working in Albania today. Chinese architects, Rama says, got a free education from the western masters.

“If we continue this kind of cooperation,” says Greca, who has worked for Rama almost continuously since 2003, “there will come a day when Albanian architects will win competitions not only here but around the world.”

Anti-government protesters on the streets of Tirana, with the art installation Tirana Moons by Austrian architect Chris Precht in the background

But Greca’s association with Rama has made her a controversial figure among local architects. On my first day in town, our route intersects that of a lunchtime protest march. We stop to watch. Greca notices many architects and architecture students in the crowd. (The Faculty of Architecture and Urbanism (FAU) of the Polytechnic University of Tirana is nearby.)

Within minutes, Gent Progni, an organizer of the march, posts a photo of Greca and me on Instagram. The caption claims she had sunk so low as to use a disabled person (me) as cover to spy on the marchers. (Ten days later, the post has almost 7,000 “likes.”) Later, in a text responding to questions from this reporter, Progni wrote: “My opposition is not to foreign architects but to the way Edi Rama uses prestigious international names to legitimize controversial development projects, often without transparency, meaningful public consultation, or accountability. This is a significant issue for our protest movement, but it is part of a much larger struggle against corruption, the concentration of power, and the sale of Albania’s public wealth.”

Rama himself isn’t happy with all of the new buildings in Tirana. He complained about one whose façades are made of stone “until a certain altitude,” where the cost-cutting developer switched to a printed material that only looks like stone. Other buildings stand partially complete, or complete but eerily empty. And in several cases developers have replaced foreign architects with local ones midstream, in order to complete buildings without meeting the original design specifications. “We need the investors to stick with the architects,” Rama says. But funding is less available than he would like. “Certain architecture needs a certain quality,” he says, philosophically. “But compromises are forced by the limitations of the place.”

The problems with his building boom go deeper than façade materials. There are accusations of money laundering and other forms of corruption, which grew louder at the end of May, when Rama agreed to support efforts by a company associated with Jared Kushner and Ivanka Trump to build luxury resorts on previously undeveloped land. It was a coincidence that the big architecture conference started just a few days later, leading directly to the calls for starchitects to scram. Among the protesters, that demand became entangled with environmental and anti-Trump sentiments in what became known as the Flamingo Revolution.

A housing project by Madrid-based firm SelgasCano

European journalist and curator Anneke Abhelakh has recently published a brilliantly illuminating book, The Albanian Files, which gives 60 foreign architecture firms eight or more pages to describe the work they are doing in Albania. Altogether, the book lists more than 500 projects. Of those, only about one-tenth have been realized. Rama says he is looking forward to the completion of many more.

But can that really happen, given the pressure to resign? The prime minister, who won the most recent election by the biggest majority of his career, says he isn’t going anywhere.

In fact, seemingly oblivious to the mood on the street, he wants to further celebrate his stable of foreign architects. Near the end of my half-hour interview, he mentions that he is thinking of building a museum modeled after The Albanian Files, with one room for each of the 60 foreign firms to show drawings, models, and photographs of their work. He said he already has a site for what he says will be “a big store of dreams, hopes and challenges, illusions and delusions. It will be a great place to go.” Its designer will be chosen, naturally, in an international architectural competition.

I left Albania with lots of questions and thousands of photos to sort through. In one of them I have my arm around Rama. Why the show of affection? A reflexive pose when the camera comes out. But I suppose that, politics aside, I can’t hate a man who loves architecture.

Prime Minster Rama with writer Fred Bernstein

→ Baca artikel asli

#4936. Yoshitomo Nara Is Gen Z’s Favorite Artist—Even if They Don’t Know His Name

Four years ago, when Terrell Brown was a college student in Wheaton, Illinois, he spotted an oversized ashtray at a local Goodwill. It featured a cartoonlike illustration of a fierce-looking girl smoking a cigarette, along with the words “Too Young to Die.” It cost him $10. Brown recognized the imagery but didn’t know the name of the artist who created it. But when he posted a photo on Reddit, a reader commented, “Yoshitomo Nara is a really cool and famous artist, this is an amazing find!”

Brown, now a vintage reseller with almost 360,000 followers on TikTok, resold the ashtray, still in its original packaging, on eBay for $2,860. The episode says something about the powerful attraction—and afterlife—of Nara’s imagery. The artist’s signature figures—round-headed children with enormous eyes and expressions that oscillate between mischief, defiance, and melancholy—are deceptively simple. They often feature slogans like, “No nukes” and “Rock ’n’ roll is not dead.”

Fans include the world’s wealthiest—like Salesforce founder Marc Benioff, a longtime Yoshitomo Nara collector who supported a major retrospective of Nara’s work last year at London’s Hayward Gallery, co-organized by the Guggenheim Museum Bilbao and Museum Frieder Burda in Baden-Baden. His work has also become something of a fixture in homes featured in AD, from the Nara sculpture in Usher’s backyard to the painting hanging in Kris Jenner’s dining room. But his fans also include Gen Z’ers who love the characters without necessarily knowing much about the artist behind them. Nara’s artwork has attained a kind of cult currency, circulating through dorm rooms, online marketplaces, thrift stores, and social media, the characters becoming so familiar they seem to belong to everyone.

Stella McCartney x Yoshitomo Nara handbag

This week, as powerhouse art dealer David Zwirner unveils the first major solo exhibition of Nara’s work in New York in over a decade (Nara’s first at the gallery since leaving Pace last year), that duality is especially striking. The highly anticipated show is poised to be a museum-quality affair. Curated by Nara himself, it will feature paintings, drawings, and sculptures across the gallery’s two Chelsea locations. Yet the gallery will undoubtedly also draw a crowd of broader Nara fans—the public who adore his instantly recognizable characters, even if they know them best as decorative icons closer to the cultural territory of Hello Kitty or Miffy.

Now in his 60s and based in Tochigi Prefecture, about 100 miles north of Tokyo, Nara emerged on the international art scene in the 1990s. His creations’ simultaneously innocent and world-weary affect is central to their appeal. The characters “look cute on the surface,” says Takako Nagasawa, deputy chairwoman at Phillips auction house, “but if you read into them, they’re not cute, like the girl who has a knife hidden behind her back.” Many collectors, she notes, display Nara’s work in their children’s bedrooms. “I think that two-sidedness is the attraction, and very relevant now. We want to talk about what’s going on around the world, but we also don’t want to talk about it.”

Usher and wife Jennifer Goicoechea Raymond, in front of Yoshitomo Nara’s Peace Head in their garden

Usher’s daughter’s room, which showcases two Nara artworks

Yoshitomo Nara, Matryoshka, 2026. © Yoshitomo Nara 2026, courtesy of the artist, Equivalence Art Agency, and David Zwirner

Yoshitomo Nara painting in a home published in the July 2013 issue of AD

Nara prizes accessibility to his art and has actively participated in extending his imagery into everyday products. While other artists have created characters that appear on merchandise, like Kaws’s Companion and Jeff Koons’s balloon dog, none have endured and proliferated quite like Nara’s, which have an underlying rebelliousness that speaks to disaffected youth across generations. Yoshiki, the frontman of the rock band X Japan, is working with Nara on the packaging for his Napa Valley wines, Y by Yoshiki, adorned with the artist’s characters. Stella McCartney, a close friend of Nara’s, has previewed her third limited-edition collaboration with the artist, ranging from $100 shoelaces to a $7,900 knit scarf. The merchandise Nara designs often connects to themes in his art, like the $12 bandages, $85 snow globes, and $1,200 table lamps available at the MoMA Design Store. (He is one of the most searched artists on MoMA’s website, says Chay Costello, the museum’s associate director of merchandising and product development.)

Sweaters from Stella McCartney’s third collaboration with Yoshitomo Nara

Stella McCartney x Yoshitomo Nara collection will be available starting November 11

Content creator Nick Lowry, who bought one of Nara’s ashtrays 20 years ago on eBay for $250, posted a TikTok video a few months ago of himself removing the object from its longtime spot on his coffee table and hanging it on the wall. (The original acrylic painting on which the ashtray is based is in the collection of the Rubell Museum, in Miami.) “Objects like this are one of the only ways to own work by a living artist whose paintings now sell for more than I could ever afford,” Lowry says in the video, “so it deserved a better spot.”

Benioff describes Nara as “one of the most important artists of our time.” Like many others, Benioff came to Nara’s work through eBay. He purchased journals designed by the artist and his “little books” about the Zen Buddhist concept of Shoshin, commonly translated as “beginner’s mind.” Benioff, who met Nara through Yoshiki, now considers the artist a close friend. He owns multiple artworks by the artist, including a drawing of a grimacing girl that reads “I’m just alright,” a totemic fiberglass sculpture of two heads in a bowl, and a monumental bronze head. “It’s great that I picked them up early,” he says of the pieces. “It’s really almost impossible to get anything [on the primary market]. The best pieces all go to the museums.”

A Yoshitomo Nara painting in a home published in the January 2018 issue of AD

Yoshitomo Nara, The Little Pilgrims (Night Walking), up for auction this week at Sotheby’s

Numerous Nara works are coming to auction in conjunction with the opening of the Zwirner show. (Nara’s auction record is $25 million for his seven-and-a-half-foot canvas Knife Behind Back, sold at Sotheby’s Hong Kong in 2019.) His eight-and-a-half-foot painting Milky Lake / Thinking One, featuring a little girl with downcast eyes, is coming up for sale later this month at the online auction platform Fair Warning at an estimate of $10 million. Next week, Christie’s is offering an eight-foot-high bronze version of his character Miss Forest, for $1 million, in its 21st Century Evening Sale, and Sotheby’s is selling his painting Candy Blue Night, from 2001, at an estimate of $5 million, and a set of fiberglass sculptures, The Little Pilgrims (Night Walking), from a 1999 edition, at an estimate of $1.2 million. (The sellers, Theodore and Ruth Baum, bought the set at auction two decades ago for just $360,000.)

For the Zwirner show, Nara has paired works from his student days in Düsseldorf in the 1980s and 1990s with recent ones, demonstrating how he incorporated drawing into his practice throughout his career. He’s also exhibiting 13 oversized bronze heads, enlarged from palm-sized clay models inspired by the snow “monsters” from his childhood winters in northern rural Japan. The show coincides with the reopening last week of the Hirshhorn Sculpture Garden in Washington, DC, after a four-year renovation, where three larger aluminum versions of the heads are on view.

Nara sculptures at the reopened Hirshhorn Sculpture Garden in Washington, DC

For most artists, the distance between the audiences for Nara’s masterpieces and his mass-produced products would be difficult to bridge. But for Nara, it may be the very thing that makes him so compelling. His work can be both a million-dollar trophy and a teenager’s trinket. And while the Zwirner show draws the art world’s big collectors and curators, the next generation of fans may be discovering him somewhere else entirely: on TikTok, Instagram, or in a gift shop.

Yoshitomo Nara bandages sold at MoMA Design Store

#4935. Anthropologie Daily Practice Review: I Tested the Brand’s New Loungewear Label

The laidback line offers cozy fleeces, elevated sweats, and layerable outerwear.

Women's Health may earn commission from the links on this page, but we only feature products we believe in. Why Trust Us?

When I think of Anthropologie, I think of its versatile wardrobe staples like denim, office-appropriate blouses, and midi-length dresses. But the brand offers so much more, from home goods and furniture to wedding collections and statement accessories; I’ve bought a smattering of SKUs from Anthropologie over the years.

However, I was only recently introduced to its newest label, Daily Practice, which launched in summer 2024. The laid-back loungewear line combines style, comfort, and versatility for busy women who do it all.

Daily Practice is Anthropologie’s loungewear label, offering styles for everything from downtime to playtime to working at home. It’s less “activewear” and more “cozy athleisure” meant to multitask in our everyday lives. Think: sherpa fleeces, sweats, puffer jackets, casual tees, and stretchy dresses.

Ahead of Daily Practice’s latest collection, Anthropologie reached out to Women’s Health to try some pieces from the launch. Over the past two weeks, I tested four styles: a fleece quarter-zip, balloon-style joggers, a mock neck pullover sweater, and straight-leg sweatpants. Since New York is having transitional fall weather, I wore each piece on its own and layered with base layers like T-shirts and long sleeves. I also wore some styles while working from home, at the movie theater, and spectating at the New York City Marathon.

Of everything in the new collection, I was most excited to try this quarter-zip sherpa jacket. A fleece is one of the best layers to have in your fall and winter wardrobe. On warmer days, I wore it with just a T-shirt underneath, but as the weather gets colder, I plan to layer it with turtlenecks and wool base layers.

The High Altitude Sherpa is super soft, easy to layer under, and versatile thanks to the side vents, front pockets, and tall funnel neck. I love that the neck zips up to my chin, so I can cozy up on cold days.

The block print pattern stands out in all three colorways, but I especially love the turquoise one for a bright pop of color and the orange contrast stitching. I opted for size small for a relaxed, boxy fit, and it fits true to size.

These laid-back pants look just as good styled with ballet flats and a cardigan as they do lounging on my couch. The lightweight, breathable cotton material has just enough spandex to make the joggers feel buttery smooth against the skin. Note: The pants are fairly thin, so they’re not suitable for freezing temps, but since they’re baggy, you can easily layer leggings or thermal underwear underneath.

I prefer the Ease State Pants when I’m working from home and don’t want to feel like I’m sitting in sweats all day. They’re also great for light movement like yoga, walking, or running errands. I’d suggest taking your normal size in these pants for that balloon-leg look—the drawstring waist will ensure a perfect fit.

Designed with a relaxed silhouette and a ribbed mock neck, this cotton-blend knit pullover feels like a sweatshirt but looks much more put together. I love the dropped shoulders for an easygoing look you can dress up or down. Contrasting ribbed trim along the sleeves and cuffs adds style and stretch.

This style definitely runs large (I ordered a size small), and I found excessive fabric around the bottom of the torso that didn’t drape the way I wanted. However, as a tall person with long arms, I appreciate the sleeve length. The mock neck is also comfortable to wear all day without itching or feeling tight.

In theory, I love these pants so much. They’re the perfect mix of sweatpants and trousers, and the material feels like a blend of track pants and cotton-fleece sweats. The straight-leg silhouette and front darts create a more tailored look with hidden pockets concealed in the dart seam. Two large back pockets also fit your phone and wallet.

My only complaint is that the waist runs very large. I wish there was a drawstring or more elastic in the waistband because they’re too large to stay up on my hips. I do like the low-rise style, which offers a more trendy look that’s great for layering, but I would size down next time. For now, I haven’t worn them out of the house since they’re too big, but they’ll be great for layering over bike shorts or thermal leggings this winter.

Daily Practice knows its audience: working women, multi-tasking moms, stylish consumers who care about looking cute and feeling comfy. If this sounds like you, the new collection is definitely worth checking out. The label has a great range of elevated basics, loungewear, and layerable outerwear right now.

While the price points are steeper than some other loungewear brands, I found that the designs have more thought and intention behind them than your typical “leggings and a sports bra” outfitter. The cute silhouettes, colors, and details make Daily Practice’s pieces feel fashionable.

Whether I’m at home watching a movie or on the go, I know I’ll reach for these versatile pieces a lot over the next couple of months. Similar to Anthropologie’s other in-house labels, Daily Practice runs large. So if you’re between sizes, I would recommend sizing down.

Sidney Lee is a shopping editor at Hearst Magazines, covering fashion, beauty, lifestyle, and more for Women’s Health, Prevention, and Oprah Daily. She has a knack for turning internet trends, viral moments, and must-have products into smart recommendations for readers. You can also read her work on Best Products and Runner's World.

Shannen Zitz is the Associate Style & Reviews Editor at Women's Health, where she covers all-things sneakers and activewear. Previously, she was an assistant editor at Prevention. Shannen graduated from the State University of New York at Cortland with a bachelor's degree in English. If she’s not reading or writing, you can probably find her frequenting the skincare and makeup forums on Reddit or hogging the squat rack at the gym.

#4932. Everybody Wants to Do Shabbat Dinner at Erin Foster and Simon Tikhman’s Cozy, Playful LA Home

The recently dropped third season of the hit Netflix rom-com Nobody Wants This begins with a picture of domestic bliss: Kristen Bell and Adam Brody’s characters cheerfully lugging boxes and eating pizza sans dishes—living together at last. The fictional romance between the witty podcaster and “hot rabbi” is inspired by that of series creator Erin Foster and her husband Simon Tikhman, albeit loosely. Like Bell’s Joanne, Foster converted to Judaism before their wedding in 2019; unlike Brody’s Noah, Tikhman is a music executive, not a rabbi—but art is still intermingling with life.

As their onscreen alter egos feather their nest, Foster, Tikhman, and their two-year-old daughter Noa are starring in a new season of their own, moving into a 1920s Los Angeles home that mixes undefinable colors and bold pattern play with the “European flavor” of an old-world estate, per Tikhman. Researching the house’s roots, Foster found Spanish, Mediterranean revival, and English influences, and the couple felt free to embrace all three.

“I don’t tend to like following a set of rules,” Foster says, “whether it be in fashion or in television writing or in design.”

To create more of a grand entrance, the entry foyer was opened up on the second floor and “guests often mistake the entry tile as original,” Pickens Dinwiddy says. The stair runner is by Stark and the vintage mirror is from Nickey Kehoe. The walls are painted in Benjamin Moore’s Soft Chamois.

Tikhman, Foster, and Noa seated on the custom living room sofas upholstered in a Schumacher fabric. “I had girls over for drinks and we sat in the room together,” Foster says. “It’s kind of like the gossip room.”

The sofas flank a custom coffee table in the living room. The vintage chair is from 1stDibs, the ottoman is upholstered in a fabric by Scalamandré, and the vintage floor lamp is from PRB Collection. The sconces are from Aqua Gallery and the curtain fabric is by Rose Tarlow Melrose House. The chandelier is by Cox London. “They handmake each metal leaf,” Pickens Dinwiddy says. The artwork is by Danielle Orchard from Perrotin Gallery.

The multihyphenate cohost of The World’s First Podcast and cofounder of cool-girl fashion line Favorite Daughter acknowledges that she runs in a “circle of really fashion-forward women who have been featured in AD and have elevated taste in art and in design, and it’s very intimidating,” including when it came to selecting a designer. “There starts to be this culture of, ‘Well, you have to use this person,’” Foster says. Instead, she discovered her own on Instagram. Foster cold-messaged Joyce Pickens Dinwiddy, founder of LA’s JDP Interiors (and later the brains behind Favorite Daughter’s Beverly Hills boutique) about masterminding the new-old home’s four-year renovation and redesign.

“They’re a cool couple and they don’t want this stuffy, old-school, fully British-feeling home,” Pickens Dinwiddy says, but Foster and Tikhman still craved classicism their way—something “cozy, young, and playful but sophisticated at the same time.”

“I’ve realized I’m obsessed with the color green,” Foster says. “The color feels a little shocking sometimes to people, but it’s also inviting and it’s also kind of earthy and cozy.” The kitchen is painted in Denali Green from Dunn-Edwards and the cabinets are backed in a Rose Tarlow Melrose House fabric.

Tikhman requested and received a dedicated drinks fridge in the kitchen, filled with clean sodas and seltzers. “Simon was like, ‘I want a Kardashian fridge,’” Foster says.

The kitchen backsplash includes custom hand-painted tiles by Lascaux Tile and the hood is copper. The range is by Lacanche, the fridge is Sub-Zero, and the sink fittings are by Waterstone. The pendants are custom.

The result is a warm, hip house where Pickens Dinwiddy, who’s married to a Brit, introduced charming flourishes familiar to UK country homes. She softened cabinetry in the not-quite olive green kitchen with floral chintz fabric by Rose Tarlow Melrose House and added a skirt beneath the sink in the scullery, a hybrid mud room/potting room traditionally used to water herb pots from the garden. The glass-ceilinged breakfast nook—an “architectural feat,” according to Pickens Dinwiddy, that was lowered into the home via crane and which Foster says “probably added a year to the project”—has been crowned the solarium. With sun drenching its slate blue walls and sculpted medallion details, “it’s a really special place for us to have early dinner with our daughter,” Foster says, and also where she pops open her laptop, morning coffee in hand.

“Erin likes to wake up, have her coffee, pull up her computer, and sit in a room,” Tikhman says. “And her dream was that’s what the solarium was going to be.” The custom banquette is upholstered in a fabric from Perennials Fabrics and topped with pillows covered in Robert Kime fabric. The chairs by Casamidy are upholstered in a Perennials by Rose Tarlow fabric. The pendant is by Vaughan.

“I wanted to do wall-to-wall paneling somewhere,” Pickens Dinwiddy says. “This felt like the place for it so that there wasn’t a wall treatment competing with this beautiful tile floor.” The floor tile is by Ann Sacks. The mudroom bench is upholstered in a fabric by Décors Barbares.

The custom dining room table is surrounded by custom chairs covered in a Jasper fabric. The 1950s chandelier is from Fargo Design and the vintage Swedish sconces are from 1stDibs. The 1970s Danish sideboard is from Chairish and the mirror is custom.

I revel in the regal room names, but the couple is decidedly unfussy about their solarium and scullery—“words that Erin and I never used before building this house,” Tikhman says. “We actually only speak with English accents when we’re at home,” she cracks.

In fact, Foster applauds Tikhman for gently voicing concerns when, in the throes of the project, her old-world sensibilities started to feel a bit too old. Or as Tikhman jokes: “You wanted parking for our horse and carriage.”

“The man cave is my solarium,” Tikhman says. “It was my nonnegotiable, but with Erin’s blessing too.” The sofa is upholstered in Forenza Cinnamon velvet by Romo and the pillows are covered in a fabric by Zak + Fox. The coffee table is vintage.

The bar in the den features custom barstools covered in a Rogers & Goffigon fabric. The table lamp is vintage, the sink fittings are by Waterworks, and the walls are painted in Galway Bay by Dunn-Edwards.

“Simon was like, ‘Listen, I love you. I trust you. I know that you’re going to make this house beautiful, but I just keep thinking that I’m going to be walking around in basketball shorts and sneakers and a backwards hat and you’re in your pajamas all day long. Are we going to be in a castle that doesn’t feel like our personalities?’” Foster recalls. “It really woke me up to this idea that you have to balance everything.”

Out went an old-fashioned pull-chain toilet in the powder room; in came modern Swedish and Italian light fixtures, plus Tikhman’s passion project: making theirs a stealth smart home.

“The bedroom is very important because we’re people who like to be in the bedroom,” Tikhman says. He issues a clarification: “We like to watch TV in bed.” The bed is upholstered in a Rose Uniacke fabric and flanked with custom sconces. The shade fabric is by Jasper and the sofa is upholstered in a Claremont Furnishing fabric. The vintage pendant is from 1stDibs and the walls are painted in Benjamin Moore’s Gray Wisp.

In the primary bathroom, the hand-painted wall mural is by James Mobley. The vanity pendants are custom.

The vintage marble cabochon tiles are from Neolithic Materials. The tub is by The Water Monopoly.

Behind peony-printed Papier wallpaper and vintage sconces from 1stDibs, “the brains of the house are so advanced,” says Tikhman, cofounder and CEO of The Core Entertainment, a music company that includes artist management, publishing, a record label, and brand partnerships. He’s giddy over no-show speakers baked into the walls, a dining room mirror that slides open to reveal a TV viewable from the kitchen—so he can simultaneously watch and chat with Foster as she cooks her go-to tofu with broccoli (“Soy is no longer being vilified,” she says)—and an AI assistant named Nicola who can modulate temperature, lights, and of course music.

In the primary suite sitting room, the chair is upholstered in a Schumacher velvet and the walls are painted in Benjamin Moore’s Mustang.

Tikhman’s shoe collection in the primary closet

Alongside the revamp of their family home, the couple collaborated on season three of Nobody Wants This, with Foster writing, executive-producing, and making a cameo as an unhinged podcast fan (her impersonations in the writer’s room demanded to be seen), and Tikhman executive-producing the season-three soundtrack, curating unreleased tracks from the likes of Miley Cyrus and Ellie Goulding for the rom-com’s sweeping, big-love moments. Tikhman is used to managing artists, but jokes that producing the show’s soundtrack for the second time was tougher. “I want to make sure that my wife and her team are happy, and managing Erin is way more difficult,” he grins. “She’s my artist.”

“I’m trying to dress [Noa] into a cool tomboy, but she seems to really love putting on lots of jewelry and carrying a handbag,” Foster says. “I think we’ll adjust the room based on who she becomes.” In the nursery, the daybed is upholstered in a fabric by Namay Samay. The walls are covered in Lover’s Bloom by Papier and the pendant is by Beauvamp.

In Noa’s bathroom, the Hector Finch sconces feature hand-painted shades by artist Sarah Blomfield. The sink fittings are by Waterworks, the pendant is vintage, and the floor tiles are by Tabarka.

In a recent episode of Nobody Wants This, a pool party at Joanne and Noah’s spins out of control. The couple balks at first, but “I kinda like being the house that everybody comes to,” Joanne decides. Foster and Tikhman emphatically agree. “We do not want an untouchable home,” Foster says. “I don’t want to be a coaster house. I’m okay with a ring on the table.”

For a recent San Francisco 49ers game, Tikhman invited friends and Noa to “soft-launch” his “man cave,” a freestanding structure lined in Forenza Cinnamon velvet sofas by Roma meant to evoke the “Ralph Lauren vibes” of a high-end bar.

Foster says sharing a home with Tikhman “unlocked my inner trad-wife hostess.”

“I’m on a group chat called Shabbat Moms,” she says. Of its 12 members, “we’ve probably hosted more than anybody. We’ll have 30 people over, and there’s kids running everywhere, and it’s the best kind of chaos ever.”

Foster, Tikhman, and Noa in the garden designed by Emily Cooper with Balanced Ground Design.

#4930. 15 Chic and Surprising Christmas Tree Decorating Ideas

Nothing says the holidays have arrived like a glowing pine strung with tinsel. If you’re looking for fresh inspiration, this collection of designer-approved Christmas tree decorating ideas has it all. From playful color palettes to chic lighting ideas and nostalgic personalization, designers are sharing their best tips for decorating full-sized, pencil, and even tabletop trees. Like a freshly pruned fir, their ideas are lush and abundant: oversized bows, classic red ornaments, blue Christmas trees, and velvet ribbon turned into handmade ornaments are just a few of their favorite festive touches. Whether you prefer a real tree, artificial Christmas tree, or even a flocked Christmas tree, lean minimalist or fully embrace maximalist holiday decor, we’ve rounded up our favorite Christmas decorating ideas to help you deck your halls in style.

To personalize your tree, take inspiration from Chanel Sansregret of the Charleston-based Studio Miles Design who purchases frame ornaments with the year to fill with photos of her children every holiday season. “For me, there’s something especially moving about hanging those memories on the branches and seeing so many stages of their childhood together in one place,” she shares. “As a designer, I love a beautifully considered tree, but the sentimental details are what give it soul,” Sansregret states.

“There’s something so special about turning a corner and finding another beautifully decorated tree,” says Emily Hewett of A Well Dressed Home based in Dallas and Kansas City. In her son’s bedroom, she opted for a slender pencil tree given the room’s space constraints. “My son’s tree sits directly in front of his bedroom window, which faces the street; one of my favorite details is seeing it all lit up from the front of our home at night,” Hewett says. A pencil tree has a small footprint, yet Hewett notes it brings a little extra Christmas magic to both the inside and outside of the home.

To make a Christmas tree feel like an integral part of a room, take design cues from the room itself. Here, Hewett adorned her tree with a collection of glass bauble Christmas ornaments that she’s curated over the years. “The color palette features varying shades of blue, green, and pink, along with clear iridescent ornaments that almost resemble delicate bath bubbles,” she says. The Christmas theme was inspired by her living room palette. “Every color was intentionally pulled from the wallpapers and fabrics in my living room, creating a Christmas tree that feels like a natural extension of the space,” Hewett says.

When decorating your Christmas tree in a room with dark walls, Renee DiSanto of the Chicago area–based Park & Oak recommends using lots of twinkle lights and weaving them into the tree in a zigzag pattern. “I tend to opt for a warm white light rather than a cool blue tone for a more cozy, inviting look,” she shares. Here, the designer used bright colored decorations, a bead garland, a simple tree skirt—and plenty of twinkle lights. “A well-lit tree will always pop against dark walls,” DiSanto says.

“Bows are such a classic, but including them on a tree in a dramatic scale is what makes something traditional feel unexpected and even a bit whimsical,” says Amy Studebaker of Amy Studebaker Design. She approaches Christmas tree decorating the same way she approaches designing a home: ”With a fresh take on traditional, layers and layers of charm, and a little sprinkle of the unexpected.” For a fun and festive look, replace a traditional tree topper with a large bow and adorn branches with smaller, coordinating bows.

Brown has become the neutral this season, adding cozy sophistication to interiors and now, to holiday decor. Anastasia Casey of The Interior Collective loves the color for its warmth and versatility, and used it as the primary color on this tree. “I layered matte and metallic brown ornaments with neutrals—think muddy hues of bone, chocolate, and champagne—to create depth and avoid a flat look,” Casey shares. While the metallics catch light, the browns ground the palette, resulting in a tree that Casey says is both elevated and inviting.

To add charm and a touch of whimsy to a Christmas tree, Anastasia Casey of The Interior Collective looks for ornaments that feel collected and tell a story, whether they’re vintage finds, handmade pieces, or unique shapes. “In order to make it feel cohesive, I recommend mixing textures and scales, like pairing glass baubles with fabric or wood. It brings a sense of personality and keeps the tree from feeling too uniform,” she shares. Casey also loves incorporating dynamic elements like ribbon or bells to add movement and playfulness.

What is the correct order to decorate a Christmas tree?

The first thing in Griffin’s step-by-step approach is adding twinkle lights to the tree, then tucking in larger ornaments. “Next, I add the smaller decorative ornaments, and finally, I add the tinsel or bows on the edges of the tree limbs,” Griffin shares. Studebaker says you should have fun with decorating and not take the process too seriously; “It can still be sophisticated, but it should absolutely be a happy and playful process along the way,” the designer says.

What are the most popular Christmas tree decorations?

Designers say German glass ornaments, Christopher Radko ornaments, cascading ribbon, festive bows, and warm white lights are among the most popular Christmas tree decorations. Metallic decorations, whether gold, silver, or copper, are another favorite. “I love decorating with metallic ornaments because they reflect the light so beautifully,” Bonney-James shares.

For Bonney-James, a timeless Christmas theme includes a fresh tree. “I’ll trade messy needles for an aromatic, nostalgic experience any day,” she says. Another must-have are warm white lights, which is why she recommends checking the temperature of LED lights to make sure they don’t appear blue. The addition of silver, gold, and white decorations completes the timeless look and adds festive shine.

#4929. Fashion Maverick Christopher John Rogers Unveils Bold New Carpets for The Rug Company

For Christopher John Rogers, the magic of a garment lies in its making. “I was thinking about preserving what happens in the studio—draping, cutting, chalk lines, thread—and the mistakes, smears, and imperfections that are left by the hand,” notes the Louisiana-born, New York–based fashion designer. That ethos became the departure point for his new collection, The Studio by Christopher John Rogers for The Rug Company. Conceived in partnership with the brand’s creative director, Will Cooper, the line builds upon The Rug Company’s history of collaborations with fashion greats such as Alexander McQueen, Diane von Furstenberg, and Vivienne Westwood.

Marker Test rug showcased in Will Cooper's apartment

Another rug from The Studio By Christopher John Rogers for The Rug Company collection in Cooper's living area

Spanning a range of flatweaves and plush piles, crafted using hand-knotted and woven techniques, Rogers’s six designs reference the abstract paintings of Morris Louis and Ellsworth Kelly in addition to his own archival runway looks and creative process. “The patterns came from something as simple as my marker tests on paper,” he says, recalling how he reinterpreted those scribbles, which date to his 2022 pre-fall runway collection, in silk and wool. “I’ve always loved the idea that something accidental can become the very thing you want to preserve.”

Whereas Paintstroke evokes a wet stripe of pigment, Chalk Line juxtaposes raised cross-hatchings against a neutral ground. Mixing faded primary colors with warm earth tones, the palette channels Rogers’s signature hues through a subdued lens, the construction of each piece adding a sense of patina. “We wanted the rugs to feel like they could be from any decade; they could be vintage pieces,” says Cooper, who was introduced to Rogers by past brand collaborator Farrow & Ball. “They reflect Christopher’s evolution and traces of the many hands involved in their making—histories that ultimately become part of the home.”

#4928. Increasing Ozempic Dose Linked to Lower Heart Risk Than Switching to Mounjaro

Follow Healthline on our socials for all things health.

Increasing the dose of Ozempic rather than switching to Mounjaro may be associated with a lower risk of major adverse cardiovascular events (MACE), according to a 2026 insurance claims analysis presented at the European Association for the Study of Diabetes (EASD) Annual Meeting 2026 in Milan, Italy.

GLP-1 medications like semaglutide (Ozempic) and tirzepatide (Mounjaro) have reshaped care for people with type 2 diabetes, but recent real-world evidence suggests these drugs may not offer identical heart protection.

It is important to keep in mind that this was a retrospective, observational study that showed an association, not causation.

More research is needed to fully compare the cardiovascular benefits of semaglutide versus tirzepatide.

The COMPETE SWITCH Cardiovascular (CV) analysis used Komodo Health’s Healthcare Map, a large US healthcare claims database with linked laboratory results, to examine real-world data from 636,525 adults with type 2 diabetes.

The analysis focused on 185,705 people who increased their semaglutide dose and 23,104 who switched to tirzepatide.

All participants included in the analysis were already receiving 1 mg of semaglutide (Ozempic) weekly.

The researchers found that people who increased their Ozempic dose to 2 mg had a 6% lower risk of MACE — including all-cause death, myocardial infarction (heart attack), and stroke — compared with those who switched to Mounjaro at doses up to 15 mg.

“This is an interesting study, but the findings may partly reflect medication adherence. When patients switch medications, it can take time to adjust the dose and find the right treatment level, which may temporarily affect how well their condition is controlled,” said Mir Ali, MD, medical director of MemorialCare Surgical Weight Loss Center at Orange Coast Medical Center in Fountain Valley, CA, who wasn’t involved in the study.

He also noted that the study did not determine what might explain the difference in cardiovascular risk.

“It may be related to a combination of weight loss and direct effects on hormones involved in metabolism and appetite regulation,” Ali said.

Semaglutide and tirzepatide are both GLP-1 medications. Ozempic (semaglutide) and Mounjaro (tirzepatide) are approved to help manage type 2 diabetes, while each medication also has a different brand approved for weight loss.

The semaglutide approved for weight loss in the U.S. is sold as Wegovy, and the tirzepatide approved for weight loss is sold as Zepbound.

“Semaglutide and tirzepatide work in a similar way. While semaglutide is a direct GLP-1 receptor agonist, tirzepatide is a dual agonist of both the GIP and GLP-1 receptors,” Amit Blumfield, MD, with Manhattan Cardiology in NYC, who wasn’t involved in the study, told Healthline.

“Because they work through somewhat different pathways, they can have different effects in individual patients,” added Ali.

While both semaglutide (Ozemipc) and tirzepatide (Mounjaro) work in similar ways, you may find that you tolerate one better than the other.

Ali noted that you should discuss any potential benefits and drawbacks with your healthcare professional.

“I would only recommend switching medications if there is an issue with tolerance of one or the other. If one medication is well-tolerated at increasingly higher doses and is effective in the desired weight-loss and blood sugar management, I wouldn’t necessarily switch,” added Blumfield.

These side effects are generally mild and may lessen over time. However, if side effects become difficult to tolerate, you should speak with your healthcare professional.

“GLP-1 medications can be highly effective for managing diabetes and promoting weight loss, but there is no one-size-fits-all treatment. The best medication depends on an individual’s health needs, goals, response to treatment, and potential side effects,” said Ali.

#4927. In This Midcentury Nashville Home a Mosaic Rainbow-Hued Window Brings Color to the Earthy Interiors

Here’s one you may not have heard before: What do Serge Mouille sconces, a Louis Poulsen artichoke pendant, and Borge Mogensen dining chairs have in common? Hint, hint: They all live in the Nashville home of Doug and Carina Jenkins, the founders of antiques and vintage furniture store Eneby Home.

One might reasonably presume the home of antiques purveyors to be chock-full of one-of-a-kind finds from far-flung locales, and while that is certainly the case here, the husband-and-wife duo’s house otherwise expertly subverts expectations.

Built in 1970, the uber-quiet four-acre property sits at the top of a hill overlooking seemingly endless rolling valleys. It’s surrounded by native honeysuckle bushes and Tennessee Fieldstone, and the sun sets on the front side of the home at 6:37 p.m., says Doug, who dreamed of building what he describes as an “art gallery” for their furniture.

The Jenkinses kept the wall of original Tennessee Fieldstone—and the original copper fireplace—but added their own flair with vintage furnishings, like a La Mura sofa, Gianfranco Frattini for Cassina leather lounge chairs, a Charles Dudouyt credenza, and a 1960s marble fossil coffee table from Italy. “It’s super unique,” says Doug. “It doesn’t have a name behind it.”

Doug and Carina Jenkins, the founders of Eneby Home, with their daughters. They kept the original Tennessee Fieldstone on the wall and paired it with an antique marble dining table that’s “huge and heavy,” says Doug. “The guys hated bringing stuff to our house.” Even though the Louis Poulsen artichoke pendant is often seen in copper, the Jenkins were able to source one with a green finish, seen here. The chairs are Borge Mogensen; the 1960s geometric painting is from France.

A well-lit workspace features a vintage 1950s desk, an Italian cocoon lamp, and a Hans Wegner chair from Denmark.

“There’s not a lot of this type of house in Nashville,” says Greg Dutton, the founder and architect behind Columbus, Ohio-based and AD PRO Directory design firm Dutton Studio. “But we had to look past what was there—a mixed bag with a new era in each room—to create a backdrop for the work they do.”

Cue the blank slate. The couple bought the house as-is—black toilets, mustard-hued siding, and early 2000s kitchen included—and embarked on a “bigger than expected” renovation, says Doug, that translated to moving walls, updating floor plans, a new roof and flooring, and overall furnishings, of course.

With the resulting minimalist white walls and clean lines, how does one amass the amount of character evident here? “When you build in materials that have inherent imperfection, that naturally bakes in a lot of warmth,” says Dutton. Think locally sourced, character-grade white oak flooring throughout; imported reclaimed European bluestone that’s well over 100 years old in the primary bath; and an original copper fireplace in the living room.

“The mosaic supposedly came from a building in Bologna,” says Doug. “The morning sun comes up on this side” and ignites a fire of filtered light within the home.

“The island has a commercial look to it,” says Doug of the Vipp system. “It hit every box. It’s sick.” Dutton helped reconfigure the space’s floor plan to open the room to the dining area. The oven is by Miele.

A Guillerme et Chambron cabinet, sourced from Eneby Home, houses curiosities. The 1960s pendant light is Stilnovo.

The guest bathroom originally had a black toilet and smoked glass. Now it features vintage Serge Mouille sconces, of which Doug has “never seen another pair,” a 250-year-old antique Italian mirror from the 1800s, and a Josef Hoffman bench.

“Flaws make the house feel better, not worse,” says Dutton. “But creating something that looks effortless actually takes a lot of coordination. We wanted it to feel like it had just happened that way, and installing materials that feel like they’ve been there forever automatically gives the house permanence and ensures it’ll age gracefully over time.”

The push-and-pull between old and new, simple and full of character, clean and distressed is one the Jenkins family is familiar with. The balancing act makes the house interesting, says Doug, and the same inclination to lean on historic materials translates just as well to furnishings.

“Some of this stuff we’d been saving in hopes we’d one day be able to use it for our own house,” says Doug. One such example is a 1940s or ’50s-era, rainbow-hued mosaic window the couple bought five years ago and stored in a crate ever since. “We asked Greg to change the layout of the wall to make it work,” says Doug. The stone sculpture on display in front of the window is another; it’s 2,000 years old, from Rome.

An earthy palette in the primary bedroom lets the landscape through the windows do the talking. The sconces are by Biny; the oversized 1880 landscape painting is from France; the chairs are Borge Mogensen; the side table is Charles Dudouyt, and the floor lamp is La Borne.

“The fireplace is original, and we ran with it,” says Doug. “It’s New Mexico style and is super cool.” It’s paired with an abstract geometric art piece and a collection of vases from Denmark.

The stainless steel Vipp island in the kitchen, which the couple found in Denmark, is “the best thing in the house,” says Carina. “We moved from a house with a big pantry, and it was a challenge to figure out where everything would go. This island holds a lot.” Here, the family embraces the “European model” of compact living–albeit in 4,500 square feet. Dutton designed the black cabinetry wall as a backdrop for the island, which he calls a “piece of jewelry.”

Like any gallerist worth his salt, Doug spends five minutes each night turning off all of the ambient lighting in the house, like a pair of midcentury Biny sconces in the primary bedroom, an Orocolo lamp in the living room, and a 1970s Marika Carniti Bollea Nautilus lamp in the kitchen. Much of the light shines on a collection of artwork that’s so vast it’s often swapped out.

“We’ve already traded a few things,” says Doug. “If we find something we love, we want to use it. It’s what we do. It’s a perk, for sure.”

As is a home that’s forever. “Our girls want to come here when we’re old with their own kids,” says Carina. “They love it so much.”

The 1950s painting is from Denmark, the 1970s vase is from Sweden, the 1970s stool is Pierre Chapo, and the ceramic is from France. “We spend a lot of time in Denmark and like to mix Danish and Swedish,” says Doug. “We know a lot about that style of furniture.”

Dutton reworked the floor plan in the primary bathroom to set the vanity under a skylight. “We don’t use much overhead lighting,” says Carina.

#4926. Oral GLP-1 Orforglipron (Foundayo) Achieves Weight Loss for Women of All Ages

Follow Healthline on our socials for all things health.

A new GLP-1 pill called orforglipron (Foundayo) has been shown to help women lose weight, regardless of menopausal status, according to new research.

A group of researchers, including senior author Hunter Thomas Hoffman, PharmD, a clinical research scientist affiliated with the drug’s manufacturer, Eli Lilly, analyzed data from two prior trials, ATTAIN-1 and ATTAIN-2, to arrive at this conclusion.

These studies reveal that this medication works equally well for women who have not yet gone through menopause, those who are currently experiencing it, and those who are postmenopausal.

The findings were presented at the Annual Meeting of the European Association for the Study of Diabetes (EASD), held in Milan, Italy, from September 28 to October 2.

This is important because menopause often causes weight gain due to lower estrogen levels, making weight loss more difficult for many women.

Orforglipron offers a promising new option for managing weight in women with obesity or overweight, especially those with type 2 diabetes.

In April of this year, Eli Lilly’s Foundayo received FDA approval as a weight-loss drug, making it the second GLP-1 pill for weight loss to enter the U.S. market after Novo Nordisk’s Wegovy.

The ATTAIN-1 and ATTAIN-2 studies involved women who were either obese or overweight and who had at least one medical condition related to their weight.

Obesity was defined as a body mass index (BMI) of 30 or higher, and overweight as a BMI of 27 or higher.

The participants were divided into groups based on their menopausal status: premenopausal (before menopause), perimenopausal (during the menopause transition), and postmenopausal (after menopause).

Each group included between 142 and 225 females who either received orforglipron or placebo, an inactive pill with none of the drug in it.

The ATTAIN-1 study focused on people who were overweight or obese but did not have diabetes. ATTAIN-2 included participants who had both overweight or obesity and type 2 diabetes.

The purpose of analyzing these two groups separately was to understand how orforglipron works in people with different health conditions.

The researchers measured several outcomes over a period of 72 weeks. They examined changes in body weight, waist circumference (the distance around the waist), and the waist-to-height ratio, a measure used to assess body fat distribution.

They also tracked how many women reached specific weight loss goals, such as losing at least 5%, 10%, 15%, or 20% of their body weight, compared to those who took the inactive pill.

By studying these factors, the researchers were able to determine not only whether orforglipron helped women lose weight but also how much weight loss was achieved and how it affected their body shape.

The results showed that women who took orforglipron experienced significant weight loss regardless of their menopausal status.

In the ATTAIN-1 and ATTAIN-2 studies, premenopausal, perimenopausal, and postmenopausal women all lost an average of up to 14% of their body weight.

This means that the medication was effective for women at different life stages, including those experiencing menopause, when weight gain is often a challenge.

In addition to losing body weight, women in the orforglipron groups also saw meaningful reductions in waist circumference. Waist measurements decreased by up to 12.5 centimeters (about 5 inches) across all menopausal groups in both studies.

This reduction is important because greater visceral fat is associated with higher risks of heart disease and diabetes.

The waist-to-height ratio also improved for many women taking orforglipron. More participants treated with the medication moved into lower waist-to-height ratio categories than those who took the placebo.

This indicates a healthier distribution of body fat and suggests a lower risk of heart disease.

When looking at the number of women who reached specific weight loss milestones, orforglipron performed much better than the placebo. Up to 83% of women on orforglipron lost at least 5% of their body weight, while 30% of women on placebo reached that same level of weight loss.

Losing 5% or more of body weight is often considered clinically meaningful, as it can lead to improvements in blood pressure, blood sugar, and cholesterol levels.

Overall, these findings suggest that orforglipron is a promising new medication for weight management in women with obesity or overweight, including those who also have type 2 diabetes.

This can be especially helpful for those navigating the challenges of menopause while trying to manage their weight.

James J. Chao, MD, FACS, Chief Medical Officer and co-founder of VedaNu Wellness, wasn’t involved in the research, but said the significance of these findings for postmenopausal women.

“The 14% is what’s grabbing headlines,” he told Healthline, “but up to 12.5 cm from the waistline is what I’m interested in.”

According to Chao, as females enter postmenopause, they tend to store more fat around their midsection.

“This is the type of fat that can cause heart disease,” he said. “So a pill that can help a 38-year-old woman lose the same amount as a 62-year-old breaks that myth.”

Additionally, in contrast to most GLP-1 drugs, which are injectable, it’s a once-a-day pill. You can also take it any time, Chao explained, making it more convenient.

“No needle, no refrigerator, and up to 83% of participants lost at least 5%,” he said. “Sometimes it’s the simple things that make a difference.”

Luca Moretti, MD, a medical doctor at Sapienza University of Rome, who wasn’t involved in the studies, added that convenience isn’t the only thing to consider when deciding whether to use orforglipron.

Obesity management should begin with an individualized plan that includes nutrition, physical activity, and the management of other risk factors.

“When these measures are not sufficient, adding pharmacological treatment can be considered together with a physician,” he told Healthline.

Moretti further advised speaking with your physician about any potential side effects, your existing medical conditions, other medications you are taking, and your treatment preferences.

“So the most useful question to ask a doctor is not simply, ‘Is Foundayo more convenient?’ but rather: ‘Among the available treatments, which one offers the best balance of effectiveness, tolerability, and practicality for me?'” he said.

#4925. Victorian Architecture Meets Mediterranean Charm in This Warm and Whimsical London Home

Interior designer Marta de la Rica describes herself as “a New Yorker by birth, French by adoption, and Spanish at heart.” That mix of cultures would prove to be an asset for one of her recent projects: the 5,382-square-foot four-story home of a family of four in London. A fine example of Victorian architecture, the building, located in the quiet residential neighborhood of Battersea and only steps away from Battersea Park, was built in 1879. “I already knew the clients, since I had previously designed their home in Bilbao, and I knew what kind of atmosphere the family wanted as well as what was particularly important to them about their home,” says de la Rica.

The walls of the stairwell are painted in Yel-01 Smooth by Clayworks and the Clam Shell Large light fixtures are by Porta Romana. The floor is made of several different marbles: Negro Marquina, Perlado, and Rojo Alicante.

The young family is originally from Spain but had already been living in London for nearly 10 years when they decided to buy a single-family home there. De la Rica decided to design spaces for them with a British-Mediterranean look that reflects the character of the historic Victorian architecture and the demands of modern family life. “The family wanted an elegant and peaceful home that, above all, would be warm and cozy,” says de la Rica. “For us, that meant creating spaces with comfort, character, and personality.”

Two midcentury armchairs sit in front of a Louis XIV fireplace from 1700.

Light brown and rust tones dominate the living room’s color palette.

Since the clients are Spanish, the project had a particularly interesting cultural dimension for the designer, as she explains: “We wanted the house to feel personal to them while also remaining true to its London identity and the Victorian architecture. So the project became a dialogue between English interior design and a more relaxed, Mediterranean lifestyle,” says de la Rica.

De la Rica’s interiors combine English interior design and a relaxed lifestyle in this Victorian building where the sunroom doubles as a dining room.

Originally, the family had been looking for a home in a different part of London, but when they saw this house, they were immediately taken with it and changed their plans. The building’s structure was in very good condition; only the floor plan needed to be reconfigured. “Above all, we wanted to preserve the spacious rooms, the ceiling heights, the bay windows, and the staircase,” explains the designer.

A relaxed breakfast setting with chairs by Vintola Studio and Benjamin pendant lights from Felix Lighting,

Visually, all the rooms have an almost opulent feel, yet at the same time, each one reflects its particular purpose. The ground floor includes a study, a living room, a kitchen, af amily room, and a sunroom, which also serves as the dining room. The parents’ bedroom is located on the second floor. A separate children’s area with bedrooms and play areas was created on the top floor. “The sunroom is a big part of the character of the house. To build the basement, it had to be demolished and rebuilt. It was very important to us to preserve the extraordinary presence of this space and its connection to the rest of the house,” says de la Rica.

The designer made the transitions from one room to the next as natural as possible by ensuring that the materials and atmosphere flow seamlessly.

The living spaces flow into the garden (designed by Matthew Wilson) and the terraces in front of the family room and the sunroom provide direct access to the outdoors. The garden features a pool, an outdoor kitchen, and a spacious dining area, providing additional spaces for social gatherings. The interior is characterized by elegant craftsmanship, custom details, warm colors, and high-quality materials. These choices highlight the grandeur of the historic home while also creating a cozy atmosphere.

A 20th-century dresser serves as a nightstand in the primary bedroom; an onyx lamp sits next to a vase filled with flowers.

“I particularly like that the rooms all have their own character, yet there is a clear, unifying design that carries throughout the house,” says the designer. It was also important to de la Rica to emphasize the scale of the house without having it feel cold or ostentatious. “Even in the larger rooms, there’s a coziness. It was very important to us to achieve this balance,” she says.

In the guest bathroom, the walls are painted in Yel-01 Smooth by Clayworks. Below the Negro Marquina marble sink by MDLR-Design, a curtain of Marly fabric by Colony.

The playroom showcases the Victorian architecture.

The children’s rooms are located under the mansard roof, which is typical of many Victorian houses in London. For the two youngest members of the family, the designer transformed the entire top floor with its sloped ceilings into colorful playrooms.

Amid dinosaurs and Mickey Mouse toys, the family’s son sleeps here. A Globe ceiling light hangs above the bed (Olofane).

De la Rica’s favorite parts of the house, however, are the living room and the dining room. For her, these are the areas where the home truly comes to life and where the design directly interacts with the family’s daily routine.

Above the large sofa in the family room, a Soren globe light from Pinch. The cream-colored Mayan Cuzco rug is from Bellbridge.

She particularly likes that the living room, dining room, and kitchen form a single, open space. “This part of the house allows for a very uncomplicated way of living,” she explains. “It’s very beautiful but not contrived or overly formal. It exudes coziness and spontaneity, and for me, that’s ultimately what makes a family home.”

For the walls in this bathroom, the designer chose Oli-03 Smooth by Clayworks, wall sconces by Vaughan, and a Calacatta Viola marble sink.

The wardrobe in the guest bedroom is upholstered with Ticking Stripe 1 Black fabric (Ian Mankin). The decorative Flax 03 tassels are from Samuel & Sons.

In the daughter’s bedroom, the rug is from Kvadrat. The closet doors are upholstered with fabrics from Yutes.

The Bert & May tiles in Garnet and Sand White were custom-made for this bathroom. The bathtub is Perlado marble.

#4924. Inside the Underground Mosques of Kazakhstan’s Surreal Mangystau Desert

Up until about 20 years ago, a faint trail up a steep hill through limestone-and-chalk cliffs was the only way to reach Beket-Ata, one of the sacred underground mosques—as they’re colloquially known—of Kazakhstan’s Mangystau desert. “There was no proper path then, but people made the difficult climb in the heat and wind,” my guide, Yerzhan Nagmanov, tells me as we embark on our way to the holy site—eased slightly by the stairs now carved into the stone. That arduous journey, the travelers believed, “was part of the pilgrimage.”

En route to Beket-Ata with Mangystau Tour 999, visitors walk a trail cutting through the steep hill.

The trail of stairs descending into the canyon en route to Beket-Ata.

As inhospitable as the roughly 64,000-square-mile desert may seem, it has attracted scores of people over the years arriving to see Beket-Ata and the four other sacred complexes scattered across its expanse: Shopan-Ata, Karaman-Ata, Shakpak-Ata and Sultan-Epe. Traveling between the five holy sites, the distinction between architecture and geology evaporates into the desert heat: Walls, ceilings, and floors are all variations of the rock from which the mosques were excavated. For pilgrims, the journey to these sacred sites is an act of devotion, where people come to honor revered Sufi masters, pray for blessings, and seek spiritual guidance in times of difficulty.

The mosques are hundreds of years old, collectively dating between the 12th and 18th centuries, but they’re new to the UNESCO World Heritage List as of this summer. The “Rock Mosques and Associated Sacred Sites of Mangystau” were added to the esteemed collection of landmarks in July.

Each mosque is associated with a different spiritual master within Sufism, the mystical branch of Islam. The five Sufi masters’ influence shaped the region’s religious landscape, and then the landscape itself, as all of the mosques—crafted in a rock-cut style that grew out of nomadic building traditions—seem to grow directly from the desert terrain; they’re carved into caves, tucked along the contours of cliffs and hillsides, and tunneled below the ground.

After three weeks travelling through Kazakhstan’s cities, mountains, and pine forests, I spent my final week in Mangystau, a landscape so different from anything I’d seen so far in the country that it felt like a whole world unto itself. Having recently visited Astana’s Grand Mosque, with its blue domes, soaring minarets, and gleaming expanses of marble, I wondered whether Mangystau’s rock-cut shrines might fall flat by comparison. For me, however, their lack of splashy grandeur is precisely what made them so compelling.

A stately arch welcomes travelers to the steps leading to Beket-Ata.

While I am not Muslim, I was fascinated by how the Sufi masters traversed this unforgiving terrain along with their families. Some walked hundreds of kilometers to establish places of worship far from the comforts of the world, spending years in prayer and contemplation before eventually being buried here. I was drawn to the way the rock mosques embody a humble yet stunning expression of faith shaped by the austere landscape, where instead of gilded shrines or massive palaces, devotion found a more organic form. Through guided 4x4 tours with Red Maya Travel and Mangystau Tour 999, I visited all five.

A necropolis inside the Shopan-Ata complex.

My first stop on the tour is Shopan-Ata, named after the 12th-century Sufi teacher. The complex consists of a vast necropolis (a large burial ground with tombs of religious figures and generations of pilgrims) of more than 2,000 monuments, including domed mausoleums and kulpytas, which are ornately carved stone steles traditionally used as Kazakh gravestones. The 12-chamber mosque is carved into the rock, and a shaft of daylight is cast onto two sacred wooden poles below. According to a local tradition, pilgrims recite a prayer and circle the poles three times counterclockwise, which is believed to help the spirits hear them. Visitors also tie strips of white cloth around the poles, the knot symbolically binding a prayer to the sacred site. These practices likely come from Shamanic and Tengrian traditions of tying strips of cloth to sacred trees and mountain passes to honor nature spirits––customs that became intertwined with local forms of Muslim saint veneration over time.

Nearby, a well offers pilgrims a much-needed respite; these sacred complexes grew around the few natural resources that made life in the desert possible.

Domed mausoleums at the Shopan-Ata necropolis.

The entrance to Shopan-Ata, shaded by a mulberry tree. These mosques were built around oases that sustained life in the harsh desert climate.

Legend holds that centuries later, Shopan-Ata appeared to Beket-Ata in a dream, offering spiritual guidance, making this shrine an important stop before continuing to Beket-Ata. Nearby, a well offers pilgrims a much-needed respite, its water an incongruous presence in a landscape this dry––a reminder that these sacred complexes grew around the few natural resources that made life in the desert possible.

The sacred wooden pole, around which visitors tie white cloth to seek blessings.

The final few stairs leading up to the Beket-Ata shrine.

Just over an hour away, Beket-Ata is perhaps the best-known of the five mosques. Founded by the 18th-century Sufi teacher Beket Myrzagululy, the shrine lies in a remote canyon about 170 miles from Aktau, the Caspian-side gateway to Mangystau. The steep descent into the canyon is a reminder that the effort needed to reach these shrines has always been a part of the devotion.

The structure is carved into the pale rock, its interconnected chambers following the curves of the cliff. Narrow openings in the ceilings pull shafts of daylight into the cool, dim rooms, where rugs are spread across the stone floor.

Communal dining is integral to Kazakh culture.

An imam is usually on hand to receive visitors and share information about the site. As we settle on the carpets, he details each of the chambers, one of which contains Beket-Ata’s grave, and another is associated with his sister. The remaining rooms are used for prayer, with separate spaces for men and women.

My pilgrimage continues into the dining hall, where a long, continually replenished table runs through the room, with pilgrims sitting on either side and eating together. The mosque provides some freshly cooked meat free of charge, while visitors leave bread, sweets, tea, yogurt, jam and other provisions for those who arrive after them—an especially vivid expression of Central Asian hospitality.

Next we drive across remote country to the Tyub-Karagan Peninsula for Shakpak-Ata, whose cruciform mosque is carved into a massive chalk cliff. Its porous white surface, which looks almost like honeycomb against the desert, was shaped millions of years ago when this part of Mangystau lay beneath the ancient Tethys Sea. Inside, drawings and inscriptions in Arabic, Persian, and Turkic—some dating back to the 18th century—offer a tangible record of the pilgrims who traveled here from across Central Asia, long before air-conditioned 4x4s and Starlink internet made traversing this unforgiving landscape simpler.

A honeycomb-like pattern along the rock face of Shakpak-Ata.

Slightly west, Sultan-Epe lies beside a deep canyon, where a stream threads through thickets of mulberry, buckthorn, blackberry, and wild herbs—another improbable pocket of green in one of Kazakhstan’s driest regions. Sultan-Epe is revered as a protector of seafarers and fishermen—a fitting association for a shrine overlooking the Caspian. According to local tradition, Sultan-Epe was the son of Hakim-Ata, a renowned Sufi poet and disciple of Khoja Ahmed Yasawi, which connects the shrine to the spiritual tradition associated with Shopan-Ata and, through his later spiritual mentor, Beket-Ata.

The stacked-stone entrance to Sultan-Epe.

The final stop on the circuit is Karaman-Ata, where a seven-hectare necropolis stretches across a low, open plot of desert dotted with funerary monuments, some more than 1,000 years old. The mosque is carved into the slope of a small marl hill, and as you descend into the rock, the temperature drops noticeably from the heat outside. A narrow passage leads to a circular room believed to be associated with Sufi rituals including devotional whirling, a type of worship that is expressed through physical movement.

Karaman-Ata, the largest of the five rock mosques of Kazhakstan’s Mangystau desert.

Reaching each site still means long drives across remote terrain, with limited to no mobile coverage or fuel services–making a sturdy vehicle and a trusted local guide essential. But after days spent traveling the region, these places begin to alter your sense of the desert. Around a well, beneath a mulberry tree, or at a table crowded with food and strangers, the landscape that seemed so stark and inhospitable reveals an unexpected generosity, preserved in the rituals of those who continue to make the journey here.
