#4923. Switching from GLP-1 Injections to Wegovy Pill Shows Additional Weight Loss of 4.1%

Follow Healthline on our socials for all things health.

People with overweight or obesity who switch from an injectable GLP-1 medication to the Wegovy (semaglutide) pill may continue to lose weight, according to findings from the OCTANE study presented at the 2026 Meeting of the European Association for the Study of Diabetes (EASD) in Milan, Italy.

The study is among the first real-world analyses to examine weight loss and treatment experiences among people who switched from an injectable weight loss medication to oral semaglutide (Wegovy).

However, while real-world studies like this can provide useful insights into how treatments work in everyday life, these findings do not prove that switching to the Wegovy pill caused the weight loss.

The results have not yet been published in a peer-reviewed scientific journal.

This was a retrospective cohort analysis evaluating weight change, treatment adherence, and patient satisfaction after people switched from injectable semaglutide (Wegovy) or tirzepatide (Zepbound) to oral semaglutide for weight management.

The study included a random sample of 194 adults with overweight or obesity and at least one obesity-related complication, such as hypertension, cardiovascular disease, obstructive sleep apnea, and dyslipidemia.

Participants had no history of diabetes or bariatric surgery and had an average baseline weight of 221.6 pounds (100.5 kg) and an average body mass index (BMI) of 34.5 kg/m2.

Participants switched from an injectable GLP-1 medication to oral semaglutide between January 5 and February 7, 2026. There was no requirement for how long they had used an injectable medication or how much weight they had lost before switching.

Participants were required to use oral semaglutide regularly for 3 months and self-report their weight within 30 days of starting treatment and again at the end of the study period.

After 3 months of taking oral semaglutide, participants had lost an average of 4.1% of their body weight, or 8.8 pounds.

At the 3-month mark, 2 in 5 participants (40.7%) had lost 5% or more of their body weight.

The proportion of participants with a BMI of 30 or greater also decreased by 21.6 percentage points, from 87.1% at baseline to 65.5% after 3 months.

Most participants (82.4%) reported at least one treatment improvement. Around 69% reported better-fitting clothes, and around 51% reported eating healthier.

Betul Hatipoglu, MD, Medical Director, Diabetes & Metabolic Center at University Hospitals, Cleveland, OH, who wasn’t involved in the study, told Healthline that, “These findings are encouraging because they suggest that some people can continue losing weight after switching to oral treatment.”

However, she also noted, “I would view this as promising early evidence supporting another treatment option, rather than proof that switching improves results.”

Mir Ali, MD, medical director of MemorialCare Surgical Weight Loss Center at Orange Coast Medical Center in Fountain Valley, CA, who wasn’t involved in the study, noted that because the oral medication is absorbed through the gastrointestinal tract, less of it reaches the bloodstream compared to the injectable form.

However, oral semaglutide can still be effective for weight loss.

“While studies show that the injectable form is more efficacious, current findings demonstrate that the oral version is also effective for weight loss,” said Ali.

Some people may prefer the oral form for ease of use and transportation because it does not require refrigeration. However, it must be taken in the morning on an empty stomach, followed by a 30-minute wait before eating, Ali told Healthline.

“Both formulations can cause gastrointestinal side effects, including nausea, vomiting, diarrhea, and constipation. Taking a pill does not eliminate those risks,” Hatipoglu said.

Hatipoglu also said factors to consider include how well your current treatment is working, side effects, your comfort with injections, your daily routine, cost, and insurance coverage.

She added that the decision should be made with a prescribing clinician, who can help plan the transition and monitor your response.

“Both options can be effective, so patients should discuss the risks and benefits of each with their provider. Ultimately, these medications are tools to support a healthier diet and lifestyle, and patients who make these fundamental changes achieve the best results,” said Ali.

#4922. 9 Best Desks for Small Spaces That Offer Savvy Organization (2026)

A desk in a small apartment rarely gets a room of its own. More often it shares a wall with the bed, borrows a corner of the living room, or has to hide somewhere when guests come over. This means the best desks for small spaces really earn their footprint. We’ve rounded up nine that do just that, including a wall desk that puts vertical space to work, a folding desk that collapses to under three inches, and several other petite options that hold their own as furniture, once the laptop is stowed.

Each pick is sized for a compact home office, a bedroom work setup, or an unclaimed nook, and we’ve noted dimensions, materials, and assembly for all of them. If you have a little more room to work with, many have bigger siblings in the same collection.

If you want a standing desk that doesn’t look like office equipment, turn to Article’s Torme. It adjusts its height with a manual lift mechanism hidden in the top, so you can switch between sitting and standing without a motor or a control panel. And while there are no drawers or additional shelves, a few divided compartments are within the desk’s body to keep your daily essentials close by.

For those interested in a work surface that can double as a vanity or living room sideboard, the Sloane Desk from Castlery is up to the task. Elevated by fluted panels and a duo of discreet storage drawers, its look is polished enough to nimbly complement non-office settings. At just over 47 inches wide, it also has room to work as a small computer desk with a monitor, and the drawers keep chargers and paperwork out of sight when it’s off duty.

This Scandi-style desk is all about taking up vertical space—one of our favorite space-saving hacks. Blending metal and wood in one streamlined design, it provides a top shelf for vertical storage and plenty of wall space to add artwork and decor. Choose between a black or white metal frame to match your home’s mood.

A treat for the eyes—and a space-saving solution for any small home—this compact wood desk pairs its clean, design-forward aesthetic with discreet, functional details: a drawer for extra storage, a hidden cubby for cable management, and a fully finished back that allows it to be displayed anywhere in the room. With solid wood legs and slats, it’s a sturdy home office addition that’s both handsome and heirloom-worthy. At $2,495, it’s the priciest pick here. But it’s also the most likely to outlast the apartment it was bought for.

This electric standing desk changes desk height at the touch of a button. At 34 inches wide, it’s the narrowest standing desk on this list. Available in three sizes and color combinations, its ice-blue finish provides a welcome break from ho-hum neutrals—and, if you’d like, you can order a desk chair in the exact same color for a sleek, monochrome setup.

The ultimate small-space-friendly desk is one that folds away when not in use. The Wohomo folding desk collapses to under three inches, so it can slide behind a door or into a closet between workdays. This Amazon option boasts thousands of positive reviews touting its slim profile, and it’s light enough for one person to move on their own. Available in three sizes (31 inches, 39 inches, and 47 inches), you’re able to choose how much workspace you’ll need. She has since followed that project with a collection for West Elm. The house taught her to weigh function as heavily as looks. “If something looks great but feels bad, it doesn’t make the cut,” she previously told us. The 36-inch mini desk, shown here in dark walnut, is narrow enough for a bedroom corner or the end of a living room, and at $499 it lands in the middle of this list price-wise. If you have the wall for it, the collection also includes a 48-inch storage desk at $899.

What Are the Best Types of Desks for Small Spaces?

For most small spaces, the best options are wall-mounted desks and floating desks, which keep the floor clear and put vertical space to use. Folding desks are another great option since they’re easy to stow away when you’re done for the day. If you have an empty corner space, a corner desk or a small L-shaped desk can claim square footage that otherwise goes unused. A secretary desk, which closes over your work, makes sense if your home office is also your living room.

How Do I Choose the Right Small Computer Desk for My Needs?

The right computer desk for you will depend on your priorities. If you have multiple monitors, choose a desk with a longer shape and a cord organizer to ensure you have enough surface area. If you work on just a laptop, consider a portable C-shaped desk that can double as a side table when not in use. If your desk is too shallow for a keyboard and a screen, a keyboard tray mounted underneath buys you room without a bigger footprint.

While most sitting desks are between 27 and 30 inches tall, your height will play a factor in ergonomics and how comfortable you’ll feel sitting for long periods of time. For example, if you’re taller, you may find yourself slouching to type. For those who are shorter, your neck could be placed at an odd angle for viewing your laptop or monitor screen. If you’d rather keep the desk you already have, a standing desk converter sits on top of it and gives you an adjustable height without adding another piece of furniture.

Branch, a desk and office-chair company that is focused on ergonomics, recommends that the best desk height maintains a 90-degree angle at your elbows when your arms rest on the desk (a.k.a. while you’re typing). Your desk chair will also affect your comfort and ergonomic posture. You can shop our tested and researched picks in our office chairs guide.

What Are the Most Space-Efficient Desks for Small Apartments?

Vertical wall-mounted desks, foldable desks (basically the Murphy bed of workspaces), and C-shaped portable desks are going to be the most space-efficient options for small apartments. Foldable desks can easily be tucked away when not in use, while C-shaped tables can also function as a drinks table after working hours. If you need a space-saving desk with storage, look for vertical storage above the work surface rather than drawers below it, which keeps the desk’s footprint small.

Where Should I Put a Desk in a Small Apartment?

Start with the power outlets. A desk within reach of a wall plug saves you from running extension cords across the room, and it gives you an obvious place for a phone and laptop charging station. After that, look for natural light and a wall or corner that isn’t already doing another job.

#4920. Detached Nails More Than 4 Times Higher Among GLP-1 Users, Study Finds

Follow Healthline on our socials for all things health.

GLP-1 users have reported various unusual side effects like “Ozempic feet,” “Ozempic butt,” and “Ozempic breasts.” These changes have been largely tied to rapid weight loss.

Now, fingernails have entered the conversation. Nails detaching from the nail bed or changing color may be more common among people taking GLP-1 drugs, according to a new study. In the study, which researchers presented in October at the European Academy of Dermatology and Venereology (EADV) Congress 2026 in Vienna, people taking GLP-1 drugs such as semaglutide — Ozempic, Wegovy — and tirzepatide — Mounjaro, Zepbound — reported nail detachment and nail discoloration more often than people who didn’t take the drugs.

When the researchers counted only nail changes that started after treatment began, the link between GLP-1 use and nail detachment nearly disappeared, study author Charles Taïeb, MD, PhD, told Healthline.

“This is why we insist that a substantial part of the nail excess reflects the patients’ overall health rather than the drug itself, while a modest drug-specific effect remains plausible,” Taïeb said.Earlier research has also linked GLP-1 use to hair loss. In the new study, the researchers explained why they examined nails, noting that nails and hair both grow from cells that divide quickly.

However, the researchers cautioned in a press release that the study can’t establish that the drugs caused the nail changes. Both the nail study and a companion hair study captured self-reported symptoms at a single point in time, a design that can identify associations only, they said.

For the study, researchers examined a representative sample of people in France, the United States, Brazil, and Mexico.

Participants included 575 people taking GLP-1 drugs and 1,329 people with type 2 diabetes, obesity, or both conditions, who had never taken GLP-1 drugs.

The average treatment duration was 18.5 months, and researchers tracked weight loss over three months.

They also looked separately at people who had lost weight, to help tell whether the drugs or the weight loss explained the changes. However, the abstract didn’t specify which GLP-1 drugs participants took.

The researchers have submitted the findings to peer-reviewed dermatology journals, where they are under review, Taïeb told Healthline. Here’s what they found.

According to the American Academy of Dermatology (AAD), nail detachment, or onycholysis, occurs when a nail lifts, and the lifted area often appears white.

Taïeb told Healthline that the study asked about “nail detachment” because patients, not doctors, filled out the questionnaire. Nail detachment was the strongest signal among the five nail changes the researchers asked about, Taïeb said.

According to the abstract, 39% of GLP-1 users reported nail separation or detachment (onycholysis), compared with 9% of nonusers.

Nail discoloration is a change in a nail’s usual color, such as white, yellow, or greenish-black, according to the AAD.According to the abstract, 46% of GLP-1 users reported discoloration, compared with 17% of nonusers. Ridges or grooves and fragile nails were also more common among GLP-1 users.

Mir B. Ali, MD, a bariatric surgeon and medical director of the MemorialCare Surgical Weight Loss Center at Orange Coast Medical Center in Los Angeles, said that while he prescribes GLP-1s, nail detachment hasn’t come up in his practice. Ali wasn’t involved in the research.“I haven’t had anybody complain of that. It’s not something we really see,” he told Healthline. He said his patients more often mention brittle nails or minor changes in nail color.

Over a three-month period, 76% of GLP-1 users reported weight loss, compared with 47% of nonusers, according to the abstract.Weight loss alone didn’t appear to explain the nail findings, the researchers said in the press release. The differences between users and nonusers held up when they looked only at people who had lost weight.The researchers also cautioned in the press release that the study can’t show the drugs caused the changes. They noted that about half of GLP-1 users had nail problems before starting treatment.

Adjusting for skin conditions and nutritional deficiencies weakened some of the associations, they said, though nail detachment and discoloration stayed about two to three times as common among users.Those adjustments relied on conditions participants reported, including iron, vitamin B12, zinc, and vitamin D deficiencies, as well as psoriasis, eczema, and other skin conditions, Taïeb said.“GLP-1 medications may affect the nails indirectly rather than directly,” said Tanya Evans, MD, a board certified dermatologist and medical director of the Skin Cancer Program at the Melanoma Clinic at MemorialCare Saddleback Medical Center in Laguna Hills, CA, who wasn’t involved in the research.Because the drugs reduce appetite, some people may eat less protein, iron, and zinc, which can contribute to brittle nails, discoloration, or nail detachment, Evans told Healthline. Rapid weight loss may also temporarily disrupt nail growth, much as it can trigger hair shedding, she said.Ali said the total amount of weight a person loses plays a bigger role in hair and nail changes than the rate at which they lose it.

The U.S. labels for Wegovy and Zepbound list hair loss as a side effect reported in clinical trials.

The labels for the Ozempic, both injectable and pill form, as well as Mounjaro, list hair loss as a side effect, based on reports received after the drugs entered the market. Those reports don’t establish how often it happens or whether the drugs caused it.

As such, researchers have been examining GLP-1-related hair loss.

A companion study of the same participants found hair and scalp changes were also more common among GLP-1 users, according to that study’s abstract, also provided to Healthline:

Scalp redness was about three times as common among users, and most users with hair loss also had scalp symptoms such as itching, redness, or scaling.Scalp inflammation may play a role in hair changes among GLP-1 users, in addition to the shedding that often follows rapid weight loss, one of the study’s authors stated in the news release.“This raises the possibility that inflammation may be contributing in some patients, rather than the problem being solely the classic temporary hair shedding associated with rapid weight loss,” Evans said.Experts have told Healthline that hair loss on GLP-1 drugs is often temporary and tied to weight loss.“Hair loss, we see commonly, not only in patients who are on GLP-1 medications, but also in our surgical weight loss patients,” Ali said.

“When you lose weight, especially in women, it releases hormones. You’re getting metabolic changes, and all these things can lead to hair loss and some changes in the nails.” Ali said he tells patients that “as the weight stabilizes at a healthy weight, the hormones stabilize and the hair tends to come back to where it was before.”

Nail detachment and discoloration don’t appear as adverse reactions in the current U.S. prescribing information for Ozempic, Wegovy, Mounjaro, or Zepbound.

Ali said the best thing patients can do is get the right nutrition, including enough protein. He said he also advises patients to take a multivitamin along with B-complex, vitamin D, and calcium.

“I would not recommend stopping the GLP-1 medication simply because nail detachment occurs,” Evans said. She advised people to tell their doctor about any new changes in hair, scalp, or nails.Treatment for nail detachment or discoloration depends on the cause, Evans said. A dermatologist can check for an infection, an inflammatory nail disorder, a nutritional problem, an injury, or another explanation, she said.A detached nail recovers as a new nail grows out from its base, Evans said. That takes about 4 to 6 months for a fingernail and 9 to 12 months for a toenail.

Lastly, the AAD recommends having a board certified dermatologist examine any nail that’s lifting.

The AAD also recommends seeing a dermatologist if skin, hair, or nail side effects don’t improve, as they can help determine whether symptoms are due to the medication or another condition.“The important message is don’t panic, but don’t ignore persistent nail changes either,” Evans said.

#4918. Many Women Over 40 Avoid Jumping Workouts—But This Simple Exercise Is the Key to Stronger Bones

And you only need a small dose of plyo to reap the benefits.

THREE YEARS AGO, at 52, Tina Tang could deadlift 310 pounds without blinking and squat 210 like it was part of her warm-up. She had discovered powerlifting in her early 40s, and by all appearances, she was the picture of strength.

So when her annual physical revealed osteopenia—low bone density—she was stunned.

As a coach who trains women over 40, Tang believed she was doing everything right for her bones: lifting heavy, paying close attention to her form, eating enough protein to support muscle maintenance and growth. But one critical piece of the bone-health puzzle was missing from her routine, she would come to figure out: plyometrics.

Many women, particularly those over 40, have been told (or assume) that jumping is off-limits as they age. “The misconception is fueled by claims of increased joint pain, pelvic floor leakage, and the idea that bones become too fragile to tolerate impact,” says board-certified orthopedic surgeon, Pamela Mehta, MD, founder of Resilience Orthopedics in Los Gatos, California.

The truth: For most otherwise healthy women, plyometrics—and jumping and weight-bearing impact exercises more broadly—are among the most effective ways to protect bone health long term, says Dr. Mehta. And beyond just helping prevent bone loss, plyometric training can actually help reverse declines, emerging data suggests.

A recent review published in Current Osteoporosis Reports found plyometrics are among the most effective tools for improving bone density and reducing fracture risk in post-menopausal women. Meanwhile, a 2025 review in BMC Musculoskeletal Disorders found that when already-active people added plyometrics to their routine, they saw promising improvements in bone mineral density at the spine and hip.

Tang is living proof of these findings. At 54 years old, and just two years after adding plyometrics to her workout routine, Tang got a follow-up DEXA scan (the gold-standard test for assessing bone density) that revealed measurable improvements, including in her right hip, the area that had taken the biggest hit, per her initial scan.

“The trend is for women to continuously lose more and more bone as they age,” says Tang. Once you develop osteoporosis or osteopenia, only a small percentage of people can regain density. “So the fact that I improved mine—and plyometrics was the main thing I changed—is major,” she says.

So even though plyometrics may have gotten a bad rap among the 40-plus crowd, that reputation is rooted in nothing more than outdated pseudoscience and fear-mongering. These explosive, higher-impact exercises deliver the exact type of loading that spurs bone growth, development, and increased density.

In sum, stronger bones, better balance, faster reaction time, and a body that’s more resilient as you age are just a skip, hop, and (broad) jump away. Here's what to know—and what to do.

“Plyometrics are a category of exercises that require you to produce a tremendous amount of force in a short amount of time,” says strength and conditioning specialist Alena Luciani, MS, CSCS, founder of Training2xl. Often described as explosive or powerful, plyometrics are more impactful than traditional weight-bearing or high-impact exercises by design.

Consider traditional squats versus plyometric variations. Sure, goblet squats—which involve holding a weight in the front rack position as you sit to depth—can support bone density by challenging the musculoskeletal system, explains Luciani. But their higher-impact cousin, the jump squat, takes the stimulus to the next level. “You have to move faster and produce force much more quickly than you would during a goblet squat,” she says.

That rapid, high-impact loading is the exact type of stimulus that bones need to remodel and grow stronger, says Parini A. Patel, DO, an interventional pain and musculoskeletal medicine physician at Atlantic Health in New Jersey. In fact, recent research suggests that high(er) impact, more explosive training regimes (like plyometrics) provide even more bone benefits than traditional strength training.

One 2023 review published in the Journal of Sports Sciences observed that high-impact jumping exercise improved bone mineral density more than non-jumping alternatives.

Bones respond best when forces hit them quickly and sharply—like the impact of landing from a jump, explains Dr. Patel. “Plyometrics naturally provide these conditions in ways that low-impact activities cannot,” says Dr. Patel. Each time you land, sensor cells in the bones (called osteocytes) detect that burst of impact and signal the bone’s builder cells (osteoblasts) to lay down new bone, she explains. Over time, even short doses of this kind of impact can significantly improve bone strength.

Just as important as their impact on bone density, plyometrics also strengthen the systems that help prevent fractures in the first place. “Plyometrics also train and prime your central nervous system, helping to increase reaction time,” says Luciani. “Fast reaction time is significant for adults as they get older because it can be the difference between tripping, falling, and breaking a hip while walking on the sidewalk, and catching yourself so that the break never happens,” she says. This is key as hip fractures have been shown to increase the risk of future fractures drastically and are associated with higher mortality rates—especially in older women.

While plyo training has benefits for people across the gender spectrum, women are especially poised to reap the benefits. Women face significantly higher lifetime risks of osteopenia and osteoporosis by a large margin. (Eighty percent of all people with osteoporosis are women, studies suggest.)

One cause of this discrepancy? Hormones, says Dr. Mehta. Estrogen in particular has a protective impact on the bones; while the hormone is best known for its role in reproduction, estrogen supports new bone formation.

Unfortunately, that means that when estrogen levels drop during perimenopause and the menopause transition, the protective impact wanes and women start to lose bone mass—and quickly, says Dr. Mehta. (Men experience hormonal changes with age, too, but the effect of reduced testosterone levels on bone is far less dramatic.)

“Women also reach a lower peak bone mass in early adulthood compared to men, providing less skeletal reserve as age-related bone loss begins,” says Dr. Patel. In addition, women have smaller frames than men, and therefore are predisposed to have smaller bones, thinner cortices, and lower bone volume than men, she adds. With that, the natural declines associated with age and hormonal changes have more detrimental impacts on women over time.

Not to mention, because women live five to six years longer than men on average, “they spend more years in a lowest estrogen state," she says, which means the internal structure of the bones gradually weakens over time, raising the risk of fractures.

Women’s comparatively heightened risk for osteopenia and osteoporosis, broadly speaking, makes them ideal candidates for nearly any kind of bone-protective interventions. But plyometrics may be an especially powerful addition.

Consider this: The spine is the most common osteoporotic fracture site amongst women, while the hip is a close second. Breaks in either area can cause pain, as well as lead to immobility and loss of independence, which leads to other complications long-term. The good news is that both regions are highly responsive to plyometric training. “The hips and spine respond especially well because they absorb the most force when you land,” Dr. Patel says.

Importantly, health care providers emphasize that plyometrics aren’t meant to replace traditional strength training, but to complement it. Strength training supports muscle and tissue health more broadly, while plyometrics deliver the impact bones respond to particularly well, explains Dr. Patel. Together, the two types of loading work together to support the health of your entire frame—and research backs it up.

A study published in Applied Sciences last year found that participants who tacked on twice-weekly jump training to their usual exercise routine had improved lower-limb joint health in just 12 weeks. Meanwhile, a 2025 review in Current Osteoporosis Reports found that high-impact plyometric exercise increased lumbar spine bone mineral density in post-menopausal women. But, the authors note that the greatest benefits often appear in programs that combine resistance training with impact loading, reinforcing the idea that the two approaches work best together.

And those widespread myths that say plyometrics are off-limits after middle age? Hogwash. “Jumping does not cause wear-and-tear, worsen osteoarthritis, or increase fracture risk,” says Dr. Patel. “When done appropriately, plyometric drills are one of the most effective ways to stimulate bone formation and preserve bone density during midlife,” she says. The real risk arises from improper progression, inadequate landing mechanics, or unrecognized musculoskeletal conditions—not the jumping itself, she says.

No matter your age, goals, or training background, it’s wise to consult a physician before giving your movement practice a major overhaul, says Dr. Patel. A clinician can review your medical history, medications, bone density status, and any underlying conditions to determine whether impact training is appropriate for you at this time. Or, if you’ll need modifications.

If you have symptoms of pelvic floor dysfunction (e.g., urinary leakage, heaviness or pressure in the pelvis, pain during penetration, or discomfort during high-impact activities) it’s worth consulting a pelvic floor physical therapist first. Conditions like prolapse or incontinence often require some pelvic floor strengthening and coordination work before plyometrics feel safe and supportive, Dr. Patel says.

Once you’ve gotten the green light, the next step is to gradually introduce plyometrics into your workouts. For most, Luciani recommends picking just one or two plyometric movements per session, performing 8 to 12 total reps of each movement, with a minimum of 20 to 30 seconds between reps.

Perform these movements after your warm-up, but before your main strength work, when your nervous system is primed but still fresh enough to generate power safely.

That may not sound like much, but it’s the ideal dose. “You want to keep the volume really low because these movements are incredibly taxing on your tissues and nervous system,” she says. “The goal is quality, not quantity.” A smaller number of well-executed reps is far more effective (and safer!) than dozens of sloppy reps.

Workout Rx: Pick just one or two plyometric movements per session, performing 8–12 total reps of each movement, with a minimum of 20–30 seconds between reps.

1. Prep your body. Your current strength, balance, bone density, and overall fitness level should dictate exactly which plyometrics you introduce first. But Dr. Patel recommends beginning with movements that build Achilles tendon strength and lower-leg resilience, since the Achilles absorbs a large portion of the force when you land from a jump. Calf raises, heel drops, and small pogo hops can all help prep the tissues and joints for higher-impact training, she says.

As you hop, make sure to prioritize two-foot takeoffs and proper landing mechanics (soft knees, send back hips, engaged core), says Dr. Patel. This will help distribute impact throughout the lower body and reduce undue stress on your joints, all while still delivering the bone-building stimulus that plyometrics are known for, she explains.

Stay here for a minimum of two to three weeks, or as long as it takes for the hops to feel smooth and you to feel confident.

2. Level up to staggered-stance jumps. When you feel stable and controlled with two-foot hops, Tang recommends graduating to staggered-stance jumps. “Start with one foot just slightly in front—the change in stance forces your body to find its center of mass differently,” she explains. The mid-line strength and increased balance you gain here will make later progressions—like broad jumps and single-leg hops—much more accessible and safer. Just don’t forget to practice with both your dominant and non-dominant foot forward, as you want to build balanced strength and stability, she says.

3. Add more explosive options. After a few weeks of symptom-free, beginner-friendly plyometrics, you’re ready to try advanced plyometric exercises, such as:

These all require you to generate even more force even more quickly, which is precisely what makes them so effective for bone health—but also why they need to be approached thoughtfully, says Luciani.

5. Try unilateral plyometrics. “One of the hardest next levels is single-leg hopping,” says Tang. Unilateral plyometrics demand significantly more balance, stability, and force production from each leg—which is exactly why they’re so effective, she says. “Try hopping on a single leg in place, hopping forward, hopping left and right.” As you gain proficiency in these one-sided movements, try increasing the distance you cover with each hop,” she suggests.

6. Don’t forget about midline and upper-body plyometrics. Lower-body plyometrics may get all the attention, but if you want stronger bones or midline, you shouldn’t snooze on upper-body variations. “Upper-body plyometrics are fun to do, dynamic, great for stress relief, and often utilize a medicine ball,” says Luciani. These exercises—which include med-ball throws, d-ball slams, rotational wall throws, and the like—help you turn, rotate, react, and produce power through your upper and middle body, which is a real asset as we age,” she says.

Luciani suggests one of your two to three weekly plyometric sessions, an upper-body or midline-focused workout. Ideally, hitting them before an upper-body-focused lifting day, like shoulder and biceps or back and triceps.

7. Prioritize recovery. No matter your progression, plyometrics require a lot from your body. Yep, even though you’re only supposed to do fewer than 12 reps per session. “You want to allow 48 hours between your higher-intensity plyometric sessions for proper recovery,” says Dr. Patel. You can further support recovery by prioritizing sleep, eating nutrient-dense foods and enough of them, and implementing stress-relief exercises.

Additionally, be sure to check in with your body. “Sharp pain, new neurological symptoms, pelvic floor symptoms, and prolonged soreness are signs that it’s time to pause progression and check in with an expert,” she says.

#4917. Pain Prevalence Higher in Women, Increases Most Rapidly Before 55

Follow Healthline on our socials for all things health.

Women experience a higher prevalence of pain than men across different areas of the body, according to a large global study published October 5 in Nature Medicine.

The analysis, which included more than 6 million people, also found that pain prevalence generally increased with age, with the most rapid increases occurring before age 55.

The findings come as lower back pain, neck pain, and osteoarthritis — all common causes of pain — are projected to become more prevalent globally by 2050.

Pain, especially lower back pain, is the leading cause of disability worldwide. However, researchers have lacked a standardized way to examine how self-reported pain varies across the life span and different regions of the world.

This new study is one of the largest efforts to date to examine self-reported pain using a standardized approach.

The researchers analyzed data from 6,125,459 individuals spanning 118 countries from 1990 to 2025. Participants ranged in age from 5 to more than 100, and 55% were female.

They used self-reported data on pain across 11 body sites:

The researchers found that women consistently reported a higher prevalence of pain across all 11 sites than men.

The largest differences between women and men were in facial pain, headache, and stomach or abdominal pain.

Pain prevalence also increased most rapidly before age 55, especially at musculoskeletal sites such as the hips, back, and knees.

The researchers found that the prevalence of pain varied considerably by body region and differed by age.

Back pain was the most commonly reported, affecting 40% of participants, while facial pain was the least common at 2%.

Neel Anand, MD, a board certified orthopedic spine surgeon and director of the Cedars-Sinai Spine Center in Los Angeles, who wasn’t involved in the study, explained some factors that may contribute to the high prevalence of back pain.

“Our modern lifestyles involve more prolonged sitting, less physical activity, and more time spent looking down at phones, tablets, and computers, all of which can contribute to poor posture and increased stress on the spine,” he said.

“As we get older, the discs between our vertebrae naturally lose moisture and elasticity, making them more vulnerable to degeneration, bulging, and herniation. Excess weight and smoking can accelerate this process,” Anand added.

In addition to pain at individual body sites, the researchers examined three broader measures: any bodily pain, high intensity pain, and generalized pain.

Any bodily pain was defined as pain reported at any site. High intensity pain was defined as an average pain intensity of 7 or higher on a 0–10 scale. Generalized pain was defined as pain occurring in 4 of 5 body regions according to the 2016 fibromyalgia spatial criteria.

The prevalence of any bodily pain increased progressively across the life span, from around 30% at age 5 to 70% among people ages 100 and older. All three pain measures were consistently higher in women than men.

While any bodily pain increased with age, high intensity and generalized pain peaked in mid-to-late life before declining.

High intensity pain peaked around age 50, while generalized pain peaked around age 70.

These different patterns could help identify when prevention and pain management efforts may be most beneficial across the life span.

Vernon Williams, MD, a sports neurologist and founding director of the Center for Sports Neurology and Pain Medicine at Cedars-Sinai Orthopedics and Sports Medicine in Los Angeles, who wasn’t involved in the study, told Healthline there are several signs that pain should be evaluated by a doctor.

“People shouldn’t feel that they simply have to live with pain. I especially recommend talking with a doctor when pain persists, interferes with sleep or daily activities, limits exercise or function, or is accompanied by symptoms such as numbness, tingling, or weakness,” Williams said.

“Persistent pain deserves an evaluation to identify the underlying cause and develop an appropriate treatment plan.”

The researchers found that smoking, obesity, and lower household income together accounted for 18.3% of the global pain burden across all body sites.

People who currently smoked had a modest but consistently higher prevalence of pain across body sites from adolescence through age 65. The strongest association was seen with facial pain.

Obesity was increasingly associated with pain beginning in early adulthood, particularly in weight-bearing joints such as the knees, hips, feet, and ankles.

Lower household income was also associated with a higher prevalence of pain across all body sites beginning in early adulthood. The strongest association was seen with chest pain.

People with the lowest household incomes were more than twice as likely to report chest pain as those with the highest incomes. This difference was greatest in midlife and narrowed at older ages.

Williams noted that medication isn’t the only option for managing pain.

“Regular, appropriately paced exercise can help change how the brain experiences pain, while mindfulness, breathing exercises, adequate sleep, and other behavioral strategies can help the nervous system better regulate the pain response,” he said. “When medication is needed, I encourage patients to discuss non-opioid and non-pharmacologic options with their doctor as part of a comprehensive approach.”

Anand added that, “The best strategy remains prevention and preservation of function: maintaining a healthy weight, strengthening the muscles that support the joints, and avoiding smoking can all help reduce the strain placed on the musculoskeletal system.”

#4916. What Is Rhabdo? What Every Woman Should Know About the Rare Condition

A woman’s death during the New York City Marathon has put the condition back in the spotlight.

At the New York City Marathon on Sunday, 24-year-old Rachel Apa of Lewisberry, Pennsylvania, collapsed two miles from the finish line. She later died at the hospital.

The medical examiner’s office is still investigating her cause of death. Her father, Steve Apa, told the New York Times that the cause was rhabdomyolysis, commonly known as rhabdo. The rare condition occurs when skeletal muscle breaks down, releasing its contents into the bloodstream, says Robert Glatter, MD, an emergency room doctor and an assistant professor of emergency medicine at Lenox Hill Hospital.

Apa’s death comes amid growing conversations about rhabdo on social media. People have shared their experiences with the condition after intense exercise, particularly at cycling classes.

But while these stories are alarming, rhabdo is still relatively rare. Yet they raise questions about the risks of intense exercise, how to recognize when something is wrong, and just how concerned the everyday exerciser should be. Here’s what experts want you to know.

Rhabdomyolysis happens when skeletal muscle breaks down and releases its contents into the bloodstream. The resulting spill can produce dangerous electrolyte imbalances and kidney injury, particularly when someone is dehydrated, Dr. Glatter says.

Although rhabdo is often associated with intense exercise, physical exertion is just one possible cause. “Rhabdomyolysis can also follow crush injuries, seizures, prolonged immobilization, heat stroke, severe infections, certain drugs or toxins, metabolic abnormalities and, more rarely, inherited muscle disorders,” Dr. Glatter says.

Exercise-related rhabdo typically happens when an individual suddenly performs an unusually difficult, repetitive workout involving large muscle groups—particularly when doing eccentric exercise, or when the muscle contracts while lengthening. The risk may be higher when the activity is unfamiliar or far more intense than the person is accustomed to. Some examples include high-repetition squats, lunges, downhill running, and intense cycling, Dr. Glatter says.

“The key issue is often not simply that the workout was ‘hard,’ but that its intensity or volume was far beyond what that person’s muscles were conditioned to tolerate,” he says.

Symptoms include muscle pain "dramatically out of proportion to ordinary post-workout ache,” Dr. Glatter describes, as well as dark-colored urine, swelling, weakness, and difficulty moving the affected muscles. Other possible symptoms include dizziness, fainting, confusion, persistent vomiting or feeling generally ill, he says. “Someone who develops severe or rapidly worsening muscle pain or swelling after a strenuous workout—especially together with marked weakness, dark urine or substantially decreased urination—should be evaluated urgently.”

It’s important to note that symptoms can start while someone is still exercising. An athlete may notice muscle pain that feels disproportionate to effort, unusual heaviness or weakness, severe fatigue, or a drop in pace or intensity that would normally be manageable, Dr. Glatter says. In severe cases, athletes can collapse, appear confused, or develop nausea, dizziness, and swelling.

Other risk factors also include dehydration, exercising in extreme heat or humidity, a recent viral illness, certain medications or drugs, and underlying genetic or metabolic disorders, such as sickle cell.

Although many recent social media accounts of exercise-related rhabdo have come from women, it’s important to know that an unaccustomed high-intensity workout can overwhelm muscles regardless of sex. Those stories don’t necessarily reflect who is most likely to develop the condition, and studies have shown generally higher rates in men.

It’s complicated. “It’s probably a combination of greater awareness and a genuine increase in some types of exertional rhabdomyolysis, but the evidence does not support describing rhabdo as a new epidemic,” Dr. Glatter says.

Rhabdo has been around for decades, and it’s difficult to determine overall incidence rates because mild cases may never be diagnosed. Still, some research suggests that exercise-related cases have increased in certain settings. A 2022 analysis published in the Physician and Sportsmedicine looked at emergency department data from 2000 through 2019. It estimated more than 40,000 cases of exertional rhabdomyolysis and found an increase over that period.

Dr. Glatter suggests this might be, in part, due to shifts in exercise culture. An increase in the availability of fitness classes such as high-intensity interval training, cycling, and CrossFit-style workouts can expose people to unfamiliar movement patterns at high intensities.

“These workouts are not inherently dangerous, and millions of people perform them safely. The risk arises when workload increases too quickly relative to conditioning, particularly when heat, dehydration, illness, medications or inadequate recovery are added to the equation,” Dr. Glatter says. “A highly motivated newcomer trying to keep pace with a class may continue well past the point at which her muscles are accustomed to working.”

It’s also important to note: Being well-trained does lower the risk of rhabdo, but it does not eliminate it. “In a trained athlete, rhabdomyolysis is often best understood as a ‘multiple-hit’ phenomenon, where several physiologic stresses occur at the same time,” Dr. Glatter says. Someone may be proportionally prepared for the activity, but factors such as unusually high intensity or duration, heat and humidity, dehydration, recent illness, inadequate recovery, electrolyte abnormalities, and certain medications or supplements can lower the threshold for injury. Rare underlying conditions, such as sickle cell trait or certain metabolic conditions, can also predispose a healthy athlete to rhabdo.

That doesn’t mean you should fear strenuous exercise. Starting at a manageable level, building up gradually, and staying hydrated can help reduce the risk.

“The body adapts extraordinarily well to exercise when intensity and volume are increased gradually,” Dr. Glatter says. “Going from relatively little training to an all-out, high-repetition workout involving large muscle groups is a very different physiological challenge.”

Exercise-related rhabdo is uncommon, but can happen when a workout places demands on muscles that are far beyond what they’re accustomed to. High-intensity exercise can be part of a healthy routine; the key is to build toward it gradually and pay attention to how your body responds. If you develop severe muscle pain or swelling, weakness, dark urine, or substantially decreased urination, seek urgent medical care.

Cori Ritchey, C.S.C.S., is the fitness editor at Women's Health, as well as a certified strength and condition coach and group fitness instructor. She’s reported on topics regarding health, nutrition, mental health, fitness, sex, and relationships for several years. You can find more of her work in Men’s Health, HealthCentral, Livestrong, Self, and others.

#4914. 7 Easy Upgrades for a More Efficient Home Office

Smart storage solutions and clever products maximize your workspace.

Our product picks are editor-tested, expert-approved. We may earn a commission through links on our site. Why Trust Us?

You don’t need much to outfit a home office; a solid desk and an ergonomic chair and you’ve got yourself a workstation. But it’s the little things that can take your workspace from functional to fully dialed in: monitors in optimal positions, easy access to frequently used gear, a desktop clear of cables and clutter. IKEA offers a wide range of storage and organization solutions to maximize your space and eliminate the friction between you and getting things done. Here are seven of our top picks that will instantly streamline your setup.

Turn dead wall space into storage with the SKÅDIS pegboard, a particularly useful hack if your desk lacks drawers. Combine an accessories set to create the system that suits your needs: Clips corral important receipts, hooks give headphones a home, and bins keep pens in order.

If you do one thing to improve your WFH setup, make it this: Elevate your monitor so it sits at eye level. This reduces strain on your neck and helps keep you from hunching. The ELLOVEN stand also offers storage, both in the bamboo drawer with built-in organizers and the compartment below, which easily fits a keyboard or laptop.

Rid yourself of an unsightly cable nest—and the annoyance of crawling under your desk to reach your plugs—with this easy-to-install deskside tray that fits a standard size power strip. The no-drill clamps allow you to move it as needed.

Make your desk light do more for you: This slim task lamp features wireless charging and a USB port, a welcome duo for your roster of devices.

It’s a digital world, and yet, piles of paper still tend to accumulate; an old-school letter tray is the organizational answer. This one comes with four cork-lined drawers to keep paperwork prioritized, and the powder-coated steel box construction means you’re not losing desktop surface area.

Yes, storing your over-ear headphones on a stand keeps them close at hand, but more importantly, it protects the ear pads and maintains the headband shape. This one pulls double duty as a tablet stand, giving you twice the functionality for the low price of only $5.

#4913. Want to Lower Your Dementia Risk? This Simple, Free Habit May Help

It’s good for other areas of health, too.

Getting plenty of sunlight each day is linked to several health benefits, including a lowered risk of depression and better sleep. And now new research suggests another perk to add to the list: a reduced risk of dementia.

That's the major takeaway from a new General Psychiatry study where researchers found a link between more daytime light exposure and a significantly lower risk of dementia in study participants. Sunlight exposure isn't currently considered a big factor in dementia prevention, but the findings make a strong case for it. Here’s what neurologists say about this news.

Meet the experts: Anna Hohler, MD, director of neurology for Northwell Health's Westchester Region, and chair of neurology at Phelps Hospital and Northern Westchester Hospital, and Clifford Segil, DO, a neurologist at Providence Saint John's Health Center

For the observational study, researchers analyzed data from 87,577 adults who wore wrist devices with accelerometers for seven days that also measured exposure to daytime light. The team then followed up with the participants after about 8.1 years. During that time, 741 of them were diagnosed with dementia.

They discovered that participants who were exposed to average daytime light levels higher than 1,000 lux (similar to an overcast day) had a 16 percent lower relative risk of developing dementia. Those who spent longer periods of time in brighter daytime light (at least 5,000 lux) had a 17 percent lower relative risk of dementia.

The amount of outdoor light exposure mattered, too. In one model, people who got less than 42 minutes of bright daytime light exposure each day were more likely to develop dementia than those who had six established risk factors for dementia (including alcohol use, obesity, air pollution, and hearing loss).

"Further research should explore its role in dementia screening and inform the development of light-based interventions," the researchers wrote.

It's crucial to stress that the study found a link between daylight exposure and lowered dementia risk, but it didn't prove cause and effect. It also just uncovered an association—researchers didn't explore what may be behind it. Still, experts have a few theories.

"Regular exposure to daylight may support several factors that are important for brain health," says Anna Hohler, MD, director of neurology for Northwell Health's Westchester Region, and chair of neurology at Phelps Hospital and Northern Westchester Hospital. People who spend more time outdoors tend to be more physically active, socially engaged, and have healthier sleep-wake cycles, all of which have been linked to a lower risk of cognitive decline, Dr. Hohler says. Therefore, the amount of daylight exposure measured may be “a marker of a broader pattern of healthy behaviors,” she says.

Sunlight itself could be a factor, since Dr. Hohler says it helps regulate the body's circadian rhythm. This influences sleep quality, hormone regulation, metabolism, and brain function. "Good sleep is essential for memory consolidation and the clearance of waste products from the brain," she points out. Your body also produces vitamin D when exposed to the sun, which may also be linked to these benefits, “although it is likely only one piece of a much larger picture," Dr. Hohler says.

Finally, people who spend time outdoors tend to be more physically and socially active, habits which are strongly associated with maintaining cognitive health, according to Clifford Segil, DO, neurologist at Providence Saint John's Health Center. “To obtain the increased sunlight exposure does require you to be out and about, which activates the social pathways in your brain,” he says. These behaviors also have far-reaching benefits beyond dementia risk. “Maintaining social connections throughout your life has been shown to be brain healthy and just good for you.”

Again, the study didn't prove that spending more time outside will magically prevent you from developing dementia. But it certainly makes a case for including time outside as part of your daily routine. But what should you aim for?

"A practical goal is to spend some time outdoors every day, particularly in the morning when natural light helps reinforce healthy circadian rhythms," Dr. Hohler says. She suggests scheduling a daily walk, exercising outdoors, gardening, or even sipping your coffee outside. These habits "can increase light exposure, while also encouraging physical activity and social interaction," Dr. Hohler says.

You can also get your friends involved by scheduling walks together to catch up instead of sitting inside and sipping a glass of wine, per Dr. Segil. “Choose an outdoor social activity...to maximize the benefits of possible sunlight, movement, and friendship, which may all be brain healthy,” he says.

But, of course, don't neglect other habits to support your brain health. "Sunlight should be viewed as one component of a brain-healthy lifestyle that also includes regular exercise, adequate sleep, a nutritious diet, and social engagement," Dr. Hohler says.

Carina Hsieh, MPH, is the deputy features editor of Women’s Health. She has more than a decade’s worth of experience working in media and has covered everything from beauty, fashion, travel, lifestyle, pets, to health.

She began her career as an intern in the fashion closet at Cosmopolitan where she worked her way up to Senior Sex & Relationships Editor. While covering women’s health there, she discovered her passion for health service journalism and took a break to get her Masters in Public Health. Post-grad school, she worked as a freelance writer and as The Daily Beast’s first Beauty, Health, and Wellness Reporter.

Carina is an alum of the Fashion Institute of Technology and the Yale School of Public Health. She and her French Bulldog, Bao Bao, split their time between Brooklyn and Connecticut. She enjoys reformer Pilates, (slow) running, and smelling the fancy toiletries in boutique fitness class locker rooms.

#4912. 7 Best Oushak Rugs Fit for Royalty, Vetted by AD (2026)

Few home furnishings can make an ordinary room feel quite so grand as Oushak rugs. Named for the Turkish town of Uşak, where the weaving tradition began, these hand-knotted floor coverings gained popularity during the Ottoman Empire and were eventually exported across Europe. Their designs are recognizable for their large-scale medallions, geometric borders, and floral motifs, many of which drew inspiration from Ottoman art and architecture. The result is a style that’s decorative but never overly fussy, which helps to explain how it has withstood the test of time.

An Oushak rug can sit comfortably beneath a traditional dining table, add some contrast to a pared-back living room, or bring some personality to an otherwise neutral bedroom. And while antique examples are certainly worth seeking out, plenty of modern Oushak rugs capture the same old-world grandeur. Put simply, you may not be a sultan, but you can certainly decorate like one.

This one-of-a-kind, 100% wool rug is hand-knotted in Taşpinar, Turkey. Its intricate pattern, light pink palette, and low-pile height of half a centimeter give it a lived-in feel that works particularly well alongside more contemporary furniture. The wool is naturally durable, so even with a little wear, the rug has plenty of life left in it.

Bring a little more floral to your floors with this lavender Oushak. Its vintage-inspired pattern is full of the botanical motifs that make Turkish rugs so distinctive, while the soft color protects its relaxed aura from feeling overly ornate. Hand-knotted from New Zealand wool, the floor covering adds just enough pattern to a room without taking over. Basically, it’s the kind of rug that makes you want to kick off your shoes and stay awhile.

This cotton-candy-hued Oushak rug, another flower-filled masterpiece, features scattered palmettes and geometric shapes that draw on the rich history of Turkish patternmaking. Handmade by artisans in India from a blend of New Zealand wool, cotton, and polyester, and available in a variety of sizes to fit many spaces, the rug’s plush feel and storied patina make it a beautiful foundation for any room.

This Oushak-inspired rug pairs symmetrical designs with a sky-blue palette, bringing brightness to any room. Think of it as artwork you can touch or, rather, walk all over. Simply place it down and let the compliments roll in. It’s meticulously handwoven from high-quality wool, making it a durable choice for high-traffic areas, and because it comes in several sizes, its an especially versatile choice.

If you’re searching for a floor covering that has the essence of an antique Persian rug, but you aren’t quite ready to worry about spills, look no further. While this option features a delicate floral pattern, its tufted polyester construction makes it a more resilient selection for the busier corners of your home. It’s machine washable up to the 8-foot-by-10-foot size (larger versions are still washable but may require a commercial machine), and is available in area rug, round, and runner formats. It’s a particularly good pick for busy households where a beautiful rug has to do more than just look pretty.

For those who prefer more subtle patterns, this simple but sophisticated hand-knotted Oushak rug is a lovely option. Its understated mix of cream, brown, green, and blue complements a soft, slightly faded finish that gives it the appeal of a vintage find.

This Oushak rug looks like it belongs to another millennium and should live in the Metropolitan Museum of Art with its perfectly faded medallion pattern and ornamental border. But it’s actually brand new—and an affordable option for just about any home. Its muted palette can skew bohemian or coastal, or simply bring a little intrigue to a cozy space of any style.

#4911. 10 Signs You’re Falling Out of Love With Your Partner—And What to do Next, According to Relationship Experts

Here's how to tell if you should call it quits or save your 'ship.

Women's Health may earn commission from the links on this page, but we only feature products we believe in. Why Trust Us?

You feel a rush every time their name pops up on your phone. You can’t stop thinking about them. You’re counting down the hours until the next time you see each other. You’ve fallen in love, and it's “that can't-eat, can't-sleep, reach-for-the-stars, over-the-fence, World Series kind of stuff.” (IYKYK—and when it comes to love, you know.)

Or at least, you used to feel that way. But lately, you’ve started daydreaming about the sexy stranger you see on the train every morning, your partner’s once-adorable habits have become downright annoying, or you feel more like roommates than soulmates. If any of this sounds familiar, you might be falling out of love.

Rest assured, “it’s normal for partners to feel a sense of ‘falling out of love’ now and again,” says Carla Marie Manly, PhD, a clinical psychologist and author of The Joy of Imperfect Love. Relationships naturally change over time, and your feelings can ebb and flow along with those changes.

Often, a change in those warm-fuzzy feels is due to a variety of factors, rather than a single issue, says Nikki Coleman, PhD, a licensed psychologist and sex self-confidence coach based in Texas. Some common causes could be mental health challenges, a shift in personal values, financial stress, parenting duties, or time spent away from each other due to work. Any and/or a combination of these factors can lead a couple to drift apart, even though neither partner has done anything “wrong.”

Falling out of love doesn’t necessarily mean it’s time to call it quits—instead, it might be an opportunity to reassess the relationship and rekindle your connection. But first, here's how to tell if you've lost that loving feeling:

Think back to the early stages of dating: You showed up to a date, eager to learn everything you could about your new love interest. When there is a lack of curiosity—maybe you feel like you already know everything about your partner, for example—it’s a big sign you could be falling out of love, Manly says.

Unlike the honeymoon phase when enthusiasm is heightened, falling out of love is often marked by a sense of apathy or ambivalence. This is known as “relationship fatigue,” meaning you feel drained and have less motivation to keep the connection going, says Kalley Hartman, LMFT, a licensed marriage and family therapist in Newport Beach, California. “Over time, we may start to feel overwhelmed by our relationships, leading us to become tired or apathetic about investing energy in them.”

Relationship fatigue can look like being disengaged from your partner, generally uninterested in them and the relationship, or even cynical about your future together. You may forget important milestones, stop prioritizing quality time together, or feel like putting in effort is a chore. “Partners who are falling out of love often stop making plans,” Manly says. “A partner who shows no interest in planning future vacations or creating shared goals may be signaling that they have one foot out the door.”

“This may show up as avoiding conversations, not making time to spend together, or having difficulty communicating feelings and ideas,” Hartman says. Maybe you give them one-word or noncommittal answers, or you share less with them about your day than you normally would. You may even start to shut down or hide your true thoughts from them, which can create emotional distance (and resentment) over time. On that note...

“When someone is falling out of love, they may become emotionally distant, avoid physical intimacy, stop expressing affection, or appear detached and unresponsive to emotional needs,” says Brandon Santan, PhD, a therapist specializing in relationships based in Chattanooga, Tennessee.

Essentially, you're operating on autopilot—you're going through all the motions but (pardon the pun) your heart's not in it. “A noticeable decline in enthusiasm and interest in spending time together, engaging in shared activities, or discussing future plans may suggest that feelings are fading,” Santan says.

Conflict is a natural part of relationships, but sometimes, too much friction means love is fizzling. “When there is more conflict than connection, it is time to look at and address what has changed and get curious about why that is,” Coleman says.

A partner who is falling out of love may also instigate conflict as a means of creating distance in the relationship, adds Manly, but it’s even more telling when there’s no interest in making peace at all. “Although ongoing conflicts are never a good sign in a relationship, a lack of interest in resolving the root issues is often a sign that feelings of love have waned—or that there was an inability to create lasting love from the onset,” she says.

6. You’re not as interested in quality time as you used to be.

Thoughtfully-planned date nights and spontaneous weekend trips may have been the norm when you first got together, but if you’re significantly less eager to spend QT with your S.O. nowadays, it could be a red flag. “When you find reasons to not be around [your partner], it’s a signal that something is amiss in the relationship,” says Saba Harouni Lurie, LMFT, a licensed marriage and family therapist and owner of Take Root Therapy in Los Angeles. Maybe you’re“too busy with work” for your usual weeknight hang, or you’re suddenly too sick to attend an important family event with them.

The disconnect can apply to quieter everyday moments, too. “When you don't feel excited about your partner or the idea of spending time with them—especially doing the mundane tasks of life—it is likely that you are not feeling as in love anymore,” says Coleman.

7. Intimacy and sex are minimal or nonexistent.

There are many different types of intimacy (including non-physical) that contribute to a strong connection. But when intimacy no longer exists, it can feel impossible to experience love. “If the idea of physical touch or intimacy feels like something you'd rather not do and you don't have much desire to engage in it, this could be a sign [you're falling out of love],” says Surabhi Jagdish, a licensed marriage and family therapist in Houston.

There are many reasons your (or your partner’s) sex drive might ebb and flow, and having sex or being intimate less frequently doesn’t automatically mean you’re falling out of love. But it can indicate that something is off—especially if there’s a complete lack of communication, and no interest in fostering other forms of connection.

“Partners who are deeply connected on an emotional level tend to move through the natural highs and lows of sexual intimacy with grace,” Manly says.

This one might seem painfully obvious, but if you often fantasize about being single, feel jealous of your unattached friends, or wonder what it would be like to date other people, it's a pretty clear indication that you are contemplating a life outside the relationship, Santan says.

Now, there's not necessarily a cause for concern if you simply have a crush on someone who's not your partner—these occasionally happen in long-term monogamous relationships, and they're generally harmless. But if you’re regularly entertaining the idea of life without your partner to the point where you think you’d be better off without them, you may have already made up your mind.

It’s normal to get on each other’s nerves a little—nobody is perfect! But maybe you constantly feel frustrated by the way they chew their food, leave their clothes on the floor, or talk through TV shows. If your partner’s quirks, behavior, and personality seem to get on your nerves more than usual, or if something you once found endearing is now annoying, it’s likely a sign you're no longer feeling the love, says Manly.

Falling out of love may happen naturally, due to partners taking different life paths that feel very separate. Say your partner enjoys traveling extensively for work, yet you constantly crave more time together. After years of ongoing separation and lack of physical connection, the relationship may ultimately suffer, Manly says.

“Loving partnerships rely on communication, togetherness, shared ventures, and connective play,” she adds. “If partners routinely take paths that leave each person feeling separate and alone, the loving bonds often fade away.” To close the gap between diverging life paths, you might make a concerted effort to be with your partner by always traveling to meet up on their business trips, for example. But if that effort is not reciprocated, you may to start to feel continuously “unseen or unloved,” says Manly, which can lead to someone falling out of love.

Falling out of love or... just feeling comfortable? Over time, as a couple becomes comfortable in their relationship, they may feel less passion and lust for one another than they did in the beginning. Instead, they develop a more companionate relationship, where they feel less of the highs (and lows) of an uber romantic feeling of love, and more of a steady and safe friendship feeling of love. “You are still invested and connected. There’s a feeling of love and desire…it just changes,” Harouni Lurie says. For example, you may not have sex as often as in the early days of dating; instead, you find contentment cozying up on the couch and watching TV together. That doesn’t mean you’re not in love anymore—it’s actually normal and healthy for relationships to feel more companionate over time. “It’s not sustainable to stay in lust and to have the passion we experienced when first falling in love,” says Harouni Lurie. “We wouldn’t be able to live our lives as fully if we were always in that place.” When you're falling out of love, however, you don't feel comfortable—you feel stuck. Figuring out where you feelings lie will help you decide what to do next.

Before making any big decisions about your relationship, it's necessary to understand what’s causing this newfound lack of love, Harouni Lurie says. Is it circumstantial, like one person working too much? Was there a betrayal that never got addressed and you slowly drifted apart?

There could be a single make-or-break issue, but usually, there’s more than one factor, says Sarah Epstein, a licensed marriage and family therapist who practices in Philadelphia and Dallas. What’s more: The cause(s) might be out of your control. “In some cases, the issue has nothing to do with a partner’s behavior and is solely related to stress, unresolved internal issues, or personal shifts,” Manly says.

It’s also possible that you’re not falling out of love, you’re just feeling disconnected, Epstein adds. Maybe you’re in different life stages—for example, your partner is starting grad school, while you’re trying to climb the corporate ladder—so you don’t feel like you have much in common anymore. Your lives, which were once intertwined, now seem to be going in different directions.

Once you know the root of the issue, consider what you need to rekindle your connection, Epstein says. There isn’t a one-size-fits-all approach when it comes to reviving a relationship, but self-reflection is often a key component, says Manly. That might look like journaling, pursuing a passion, or seeing a therapist.

That includes assessing shared values and long-term goals. “Determine whether the issues you are facing are temporary, or if they point to deeper incompatibilities that may be difficult to overcome,” Santan says. “This assessment can help you gain clarity on whether the relationship is salvageable, or if it may be healthier for both parties to move on.”

It can be tough to admit you’re falling out of love, and even trickier to communicate that to your partner. Even so, Santan suggests initiating an honest, compassionate conversation with them about your feelings. “Choose a suitable time and place to express your thoughts, concerns, and doubts,” he says. “Clearly communicate your emotional state and be open to listening to their perspective as well. Keep in mind that effective communication is key to understanding each other and finding potential solutions.”

You might say something like, “Hey, there’s something I’ve been wanting to talk to you about. Sometimes, it feels like our connection has changed… do you ever feel that way, too?” Admitting something super vulnerable—and potentially very hurtful—can be intimidating, but it's a necessary to get to the root of the issue. Plus, if your partner has been feeling the same way, the conversation can be a healing experience for you both.

From there, you've got options: You can decide that the relationship is repairable, call it quits, take a break to reassess further, or seek couples therapy to help you navigate the situation. “Based on the self-reflection, communication, and professional guidance, you will eventually need to make a decision about the future of your relationship,” Santan says. “Remember that this decision should be made with careful consideration of your own well-being and the well-being of your partner.”

“Falling back in love takes time and effort from both partners. It may not happen overnight, and patience, understanding, and commitment are key,” Santan says. “If both individuals are willing to put in the work and are open to growth, it is possible to rediscover the love and connection that initially brought them together.”

Remember, as you both grow and change as people, so must your relationship. “The goal is to find new ways to rediscover each other, and pursue the experience of being in love with them over and over again,” says Coleman.

“Take some time to ask yourself, ‘What attracted me to my partner? What helped me fall in love in the first place? What are some things I said or did but don't anymore? What are some things they said or did but don't do anymore? Are these things I still experience or notice with my partner?’” Jagdish says. “If you can identify these things, look for them in your relationship again.”

It all comes back to getting curious about your partner. Imagine going on a familiar hike—with an incurious mindset, you may not be as interested in your surroundings because you’ve been down this path before. However, “if you have a more curious mindset, you might say to yourself, ‘I know I’ve done this before, but it has evolved since the last time I’ve been here. There’s more to see, learn and cherish,’” Manly says. Maybe a boulder hasn’t moved since your last trek, but this time, you notice the way the mica in the rock sparkles in the sunlight, helping you appreciate its beauty all over again.

It’s useful for couples to think of their relationship not as one relationship, but as many different versions. “What your relationship looks like at 20 isn’t going to be what it looks like at 40, 60, etc.,” says Epstein. “Beliefs, values, trauma, all of these things will change your relationship over time.”

Instead of thinking, “How can we get back to what our relationship was?” reframing the question to: How can this relationship work for us now? What can our love and connection look like going forward?

Ultimately, whether you decide to rebuild or end the relationship, know that you deserve happiness and all the joy that lasting love can bring.
